<meta>This page marks the beginning of the Shiva Samhita, a foundational Sanskrit text on Hatha Yoga. The translation maintains the philosophical tone of the original, where Shiva (the speaker) addresses the nature of reality and the purpose of Yoga.</meta>

->[An emblem featuring a six-pointed star, or <term>Shatkona|A symbol representing the union of masculine and feminine divine energies</term>, with the word "SRIS" and the Sanskrit phrase "Soham" <note>original: सोऽहं; meaning "I am He" or "I am That," signifying the identity of the individual self with the universal spirit</note>.]<-

# THE COMPILATION OF SHIVA (SIVA SAMHITA)

## CHAPTER I

### Existence is one alone

**Knowledge is one, eternal, and without beginning or end; no other true substance exists. The divisions perceived in this world arise only through the limitations of our senses; when these limitations are removed, this Knowledge alone shines forth, and nothing else.** <note>original Sanskrit: "Ekam jñānam nityam..." The text opens by establishing a non-dualist (Advaita) perspective where only pure consciousness is real.</note>

**2-3. Now, out of love for my devotees, I—the Lord <note>original: <term>Ishvara|The Supreme Being or Personal God</term></note> of all beings and the giver of spiritual liberation—declare the instruction of Yoga <note>original: <term>Yoganushasana|The systematic, traditional teaching of Yoga</term></note>. I cast aside the conflicting views of those who argue from false premises. This teaching is for the spiritual freedom of those whose minds are focused solely on Me.**

### Differences of opinion

**4. Some people praise truth; others value purification and physical discipline <note>original: <term>Tapas|Ascetic practices or "heat-generating" spiritual efforts</term></note>. Some praise forgiveness, while others value equality and honesty.**

**5. Some praise the giving of charity, others honor the rituals performed for ancestors; some value outward action <note>original: <term>Karma|Ritual or religious works</term></note>, while others believe that the highest path is total dispassion <note>original: <term>Vairâgya|The state of being detached from worldly desires</term></note>.**

<summary>This introductory page of the Shiva Samhita establishes that supreme knowledge (Jnana) is the only eternal reality and announces Shiva's intent to teach Yoga to those seeking liberation. It lists various worldly and religious paths—such as asceticism, ritual, and charity—which the text will contrast with the path of Yoga.</summary>

<keywords>Shiva Samhita, Jnana, Ishvara, Yoganushasana, Karma, Vairagya, Shatkona, Non-dualism, Yoga philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory section of the Shiva Samhita, transitioning from the initial invocation to a survey of the various—and often conflicting—philosophical and religious paths found in ancient India.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

# THE SHIVA SAMHITA

---

->Some wise people praise the performance of the duties of a householder;<-
->others believe that rituals, such as the fire-sacrifice, are the highest path.<-
<note>original: "अग्निहोत्रादिकं कर्म" (agnihotradikam karma). The fire-sacrifice refers to the Agnihotra, a foundational Vedic ritual involving offerings into a sacred flame.</note>

6. Some wise persons praise the performance of the duties of the householder; other authorities hold up fire-sacrifice etc., as the highest.

->Some praise the **Yoga of Sacred Sound**; others the visiting of holy places.<-
->Thus, many different methods are declared for the sake of liberation.<-
<note>original: "मन्त्रयोगं" (mantra yogam). This practice involves the rhythmic repetition of sacred sounds or prayers to focus the mind and reach higher states of consciousness.</note>

7. Some praise *Mantra Yoga*, others the frequenting of places of pilgrimage. Thus diverse are the ways which people declare for emancipation.

->Being thus engaged in different ways in this world, even those people<-
->who know the difference between right and wrong actions—<-
->though they are free from sin—still fall into confusion.<-

8. Being thus diversely engaged in this world, even those who still know what actions are good and what evil, though free from sin, become subject to bewilderment.

->Those who follow these various doctrines, having acquired both good and bad karma,<-
->wander helplessly through this world in a constant cycle of birth and death.<-
<note>The "cycle of birth and death" refers to Samsara, the continuous journey of the soul through different lives governed by the law of cause and effect.</note>

9. Persons who follow these doctrines, having committed good and bad actions, constantly wander in the worlds, in the cycle of births and deaths, bound by dire necessity.

->Other great thinkers, who are devoted to seeing hidden truths,<-
->declare that souls are many, eternal, and exist everywhere.<-

10. Others, wiser among the many, and eagerly devoted to the investigation of the occult, declare that the souls are many and eternal, and omnipresent.

->Others, with firm minds, say: "There is nothing other than<-
->that which can be perceived by the senses.<-
->How can things like heaven exist?"<-
<note>This refers to the Charvaka or materialist school of Indian philosophy, which rejected anything that could not be empirically proven.</note>

11. Others say, — "Only those things can be said to exist which are perceived through the senses and nothing besides them; where is heaven or hell?" Such is their firm belief.

->Others believe the world is a stream of consciousness; some say the Void is supreme.<-
->Others believe there are two fundamental truths: Matter and Spirit.<-

12. Others believe the world to be a current of consciousness and no material entity; some call the void as the greatest. Others believe in two essences—Matter (<term>Prakriti: The primal, creative energy or nature of the universe.</term>) and Spirit (<term>Puruṣa: The eternal, silent witness or pure consciousness.</term>).

<summary>This page lists several competing philosophical and religious viewpoints of the time, including ritualism, materialism, and dualism, noting that these conflicting paths often lead to confusion rather than liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Householder duties, fire-sacrifice, Mantra Yoga, pilgrimage, liberation, karma, reincarnation, materialism, Prakriti, Purusha</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>Continuity from Page 2: After discussing the various contradictory views held by different schools of thought, the text now transitions to identifying these as sources of confusion and presents Yoga as the definitive solution.</meta>

## CHAPTER I. 13-19.

13-14. Thus believing in widely different doctrines, with faces turned away from the **ultimate truth** <note>original: *paramārtha*</note>, they think, according to their understanding and education, that this universe is **without God** <note>original: *nirīśvara*</note>; others believe there is a **God** <note>original: *seśvara*</note>, basing their assertions on various irrefutable arguments founded on texts, declaring a difference between the soul and God, and anxious to establish the existence of God.

15-16. These and many other sages with various different names have been declared in the **scriptures** <term>Sâstras: ancient authoritative texts or treatises on religious and legal duty</term> as leaders who lead the human mind into delusion. It is not possible to describe fully the doctrines of these persons so fond of quarrel and contention; people thus wander in this universe, being driven away from the **path of liberation** <note>original: *mukti-mārga*</note>.

->*Yoga is the only true method.*<-

17. Having studied all the scriptures and having pondered over them well, again and again, this **Science of Yoga** <note>original: *yoga śâstra*</note> has been found to be the only true and firm doctrine.

18. Since by **Yoga** <term>Yoga: derived from the root 'yuj,' meaning to yoke or unite; here referring to the practice of spiritual union</term> all this is certainly known, every effort should be made to acquire it. What is the necessity, then, of any other doctrines?

19. This **Science of Yoga**, now being declared by us, is a very secret doctrine, only to be revealed to a high-souled, pious devotee throughout the **three worlds** <note>The "three worlds" (Triloka) traditionally refer to Earth, the atmosphere/heaven, and the underworld or celestial realms</note>.

<summary>This page critiques the confusion caused by conflicting philosophical and religious debates, ultimately presenting the practice of Yoga as the only reliable path to absolute knowledge and spiritual freedom.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga, Liberation, Scriptures, Atheism, Theism, Emancipation, Shiva Samhita, Secret Doctrine</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical introduction of the Siva Samhita, transitioning from the nature of existence to the specific mechanics of human action and its consequences according to the Vedic tradition.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA

### *The Path of Action* <note>original: Karma Kânda</note>

->**The Veda is considered to be of two parts: the path of ritual action and the path of wisdom. (20)**<-
<note>original: karmakāṇḍaṃ jñānakāṇḍamiti vedo dvidhā mataḥ</note>
->**There are two distinct divisions within both the path of wisdom and the path of action. (20)**<-
<note>original: bhavati dvividho bhedo jñānakāṇḍasya karmaṇaḥ</note>

20. There are two systems (as found in the <term>Vedas</term> <note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note>): the <term>Path of Action</term> <note>original: Karma Kânda; the portion of the Vedas dealing with ceremonial acts and sacrificial rites</note> and the <term>Path of Wisdom</term> <note>original: Jñâna Kânda; the portion focusing on spiritual knowledge and the nature of reality</note>. These two paths are each further subdivided into two parts.

->**The path of action is twofold, consisting of prohibitions and injunctions. (21)**<-
<note>original: dvividhaḥ karmakāṇḍaḥ syānniṣedhavidhipūrvakaḥ</note>

21. The <term>Path of Action</term> is twofold—consisting of <term>injunctions</term> <note>vidhi; positive rules and commands for what one should do</note> and <term>prohibitions</term> <note>niṣedha; negative rules for what one must avoid</note>.

->**By performing prohibited acts, sin is certainly produced.**<-
->**By performing acts according to the rules, merit is certainly produced. (22)**<-

22. Prohibited acts, when performed, will certainly bring forth <term>sin</term> <note>pâpa; spiritual demerit that leads to suffering</note>; from the performance of enjoined acts, there certainly results <term>merit</term> <note>punya; spiritual credit that leads to happiness</note>.

->**The collection of injunctions is threefold: regular, occasional, and optional.**<-
->**By failing to perform regular rites, sin is incurred; from the optional and occasional, there is a specific result. (23)**<-

23. The injunctions are threefold—<term>regular</term> <note>original: nitya; daily obligatory duties such as morning prayers</note>, <term>occasional</term> <note>original: naimittika; duties performed at specific times, such as during an eclipse or after a birth</note>, and <term>optional</term> <note>original: kâmya; rites performed to achieve a specific personal desire, like wealth or rain</note>. By the non-performance of <term>regular</term> or daily rites, sin is accumulated; however, by their performance, no extra merit is gained <note>In Vedic philosophy, regular duties are like "maintenance"—you are expected to do them, and only failing to do them causes a problem</note>. On the other hand, the occasional and optional duties, whether they are performed or left undone, produce specific merit or demerit.

->**The fruits of actions should be known as twofold: heaven and hell.**<-
->**Heaven is of many kinds, and hell likewise. (24)**<-

24. The fruits <note>the results or consequences</note> of actions are twofold—heaven or hell. There are various levels and kinds of heavens, and the hells are equally diverse.

->**Good deeds lead to heaven, and sinful deeds lead to hell.**<-
->**Creation is the natural outcome of action; it cannot be otherwise. (25)**<-

25. Good actions are truly the cause of heaven, and sinful deeds are truly the cause of hell; the entire creation is the natural outcome of <term>Karma</term> <note>the law of cause and effect where every action has a corresponding reaction</note> and nothing else.

->**Living beings experience various pleasures in heaven.**<-
->**Various intolerable pains are suffered in hell. (26)**<-

26. Creatures enjoy many pleasures in heaven; likewise, many intolerable pains are suffered in hell.

->**Pain results from sinful action; happiness results from good action.**<-
->**Therefore, for the sake of happiness, people constantly perform various good deeds. (27)**<-

27. Pain results from sinful acts, while happiness results from good acts. For the sake of seeking happiness, people constantly perform various good actions.

<vocab>Path of Action, Path of Wisdom, The Vedas, regular rites, occasional rites, optional rites, Karma, merit, sin, heaven, hell</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the "Path of Action" (Karma Kanda), detailing how the Vedas classify human behavior into positive commands and prohibitions. It outlines how different types of rituals and moral choices lead to the accumulation of merit or sin, ultimately resulting in experiences of heaven or hell.</summary>
<keywords>Karma Kanda, Veda, Jnana Kanda, ritualism, merit, sin, nitya karma, naimittika karma, kamya karma, heaven, hell, causality</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of the soul's bondage through action and the necessity of transitioning toward the path of higher knowledge.</meta>

# CHAPTER I. 28-33.

---

->When the experience of the fruits of sin comes to an end, rebirth certainly occurs; when the experience of the fruits of merit ends, the result is surely no different. 28.<-
<note>original: "पापभोगावसाने तु पुनर्जन्म भवेत्खलु । पुण्यभोगावसाने तु नान्यथा भवति ध्रुवम् ॥ २८ ॥"</note>

28. When the sufferings for evil actions are gone through, then there take place re-births certainly; when the fruits of good actions have been exhausted, then also, verily, the result is the same. <note>The text suggests that both "good" and "bad" deeds keep the soul trapped in the cycle of reincarnation, much like a chain made of gold binds just as effectively as one made of iron.</note>

->Even in heaven there is the experience of pain, caused by witnessing the superior prosperity of others; therefore, there is no doubt that this entire universe is full of sorrow. 29.<-
<note>original: "स्वर्गेऽपि दुःखसंभोगः परश्रीदर्शनादिषु । ततो दुःखमिदं सर्वं भवेन्नास्त्यत्र संशयः ॥ २९ ॥"</note>

29. Even in heaven there is experiencing of pain by seeing the higher enjoyment of others; verily, there is no doubt of it that this whole universe is full of sorrow. <note>In Indian philosophy, even the heavens are temporary states. Envy of those with higher status creates suffering even in "paradise."</note>

->The classifiers of <term>Karma</term>—the law of cause and effect—have described it as being of two kinds: merit and sin. This bondage, consisting of merit and sin, attaches to embodied souls in due order. 30.<-
<note>original: "तत्कर्मकल्पकैः प्रोक्तं पुण्यं पापमिति द्विधा । पुण्यपापमयो बन्धो देहिनां भवति क्रमात् ॥ ३० ॥"</note>

30. The classifiers of <term>Karma</term> <note>action and its consequences</note> have divided it into two parts: good and bad actions; they are the veritable bondage of the embodied souls each in its turn.

->One who is indifferent to the fruits of actions in this world or the next should renounce all actions performed for the sake of their results; having likewise abandoned the daily and <term>occasional</term> rites, such a person should engage in the practice of Yoga. 31.<-
<note>original: "इहामुत्र फलद्वेषी सफलं कर्म संत्यजेत् । नित्यनैमित्तिकं संज्ञं त्यक्त्वा योगे प्रवर्त्तते ॥ ३१ ॥"</note>

31. Those who are not desirous of enjoying the fruits of their actions in this or next world, should renounce all actions which are done with an eye to their fruits, and having similarly discarded the attachment for the daily and the <term>naimittika</term> <note>rites performed for specific occasions or reasons</note> acts, should employ themselves in the practice of Yoga.

### *The Section of Knowledge*
<note>original: "Jñâna Kâṇḍa"</note>

->The wise practitioner of Yoga, having understood the true nature of the <term>Section of Ritual Works</term>, should renounce it; having abandoned both merit and sin, they must proceed to the <term>Section of Knowledge</term>. 32.<-
<note>original: "कर्मकाण्डस्य माहात्म्यं ज्ञात्वा योगी त्यजेत्सुधीः । पुण्यपापद्वयं त्यक्त्वा ज्ञानकाण्डे प्रवर्त्तते ॥ ३२ ॥"</note>

32. The wise <term>Yogi</term> <note>a practitioner of Yoga</note>, having realised the truth of <term>Karma Kâṇḍa</term> <note>the portion of the Vedas dealing with rituals and works</note>, should renounce them; and having left both virtue and vice, he must engage in <term>Jñâna Kâṇḍa</term> <note>the portion of the Vedas dealing with spiritual wisdom</note>.

->The sacred scripture which says, "The Self, indeed, should be seen, heard," and so forth, should be served with great effort; it is the giver of liberation and the source of true understanding. 33.<-
<note>original: "आत्मा वारेतु द्रष्टव्यः श्रोतव्येत्यादि यच्छ्रुतिः ॥ सा सेव्या तत्प्रयत्नेन मुक्तिदा हेतुदायिनी ॥ ३३ ॥"</note>

33. The <term>Vedic</term> <note>referring to the Vedas, the oldest scriptures of Hinduism</note> texts—"The spirit ought to be seen,"—"About it one must hear," &c., are the real saviours and givers of true knowledge. They must be studied with great care.

->He who directs the movement of the intellect toward both sins and merits; "I am He" proceeds from me, and from me the entire world of moving and unmoving things is born. 34.<-
->Everything is seen through me, and everything dissolves into me. I am not different from it, and there is nothing in this world that is different from me. 34.<-
<note>original: "दुरितेषु च पुण्येषु यो धीवृत्तिं प्रचोदयात् । सोऽहं प्रवर्त्तते मत्तो जगत्सर्वं चराचरम् ॥ सर्वं च दृश्यते मत्तः सर्वं च मयि लीयते । न तद्भिन्नोऽहमस्मीह मद्भिन्नो न तु किंचन ॥ ३४ ॥"</note>

<summary>This page describes how even "good" actions and heavenly rewards keep a soul bound to the cycle of rebirth, urging the seeker to move beyond ritualism (Karma Kanda) toward the path of direct spiritual knowledge (Jnana Kanda).</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Jnana Kanda, Karma Kanda, Yoga, naimittika rites, Vedic scripture, rebirth, sorrow, non-duality, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the Self (Atman) and the illusory nature of the material world, transitioning from the definition of the "I" to classical metaphors used in Indian philosophy to explain non-duality.</meta>

### SIVA SAMHITA.

34. That Intelligence, which incites the functions into the paths of virtue or vice, am **I**. All this universe, moveable and immoveable, is from me; all things are preserved by me; all are absorbed into me at the time of the *great dissolution* <note>original: "pralaya"—the periodic destruction of the cosmos in Hindu cosmology</note>; because there exists nothing but spirit and I am that spirit.— There exists nothing else.

->Just as in countless vessels filled with water,<-
->The reflection of the single sun appears as many, and no difference is seen there;<-
->Whatever number is attributed to the vessels,<-
->That number appears to belong to the sun; so it is with the Self.<-

35. As in innumerable cups full of water, many reflections of the sun are seen, but the substance is the same; similarly individuals, like cups, are innumerable, but the vivifying spirit, like the sun, is one.

->Just as a single dreamer is seen as many forms in a dream,<-
->But upon waking, remains only one; so the one world appears as many.<-

36. As in a dream the one soul creates many objects by mere willing; but on awaking everything vanishes but the one soul; so is this universe.

->Just as the concept of a snake is seen in a rope, or the illusion of silver in a shell;<-
->Even so, this entire universe is superimposed upon the Supreme Self.<-

37. As through illusion a rope appears like a snake, or pearl-shell like silver; similarly, all this universe is superimposed in the <term>Paramâtmâ</term> <note>The Universal Spirit or "Supreme Self" that exists beyond the individual ego.</note>

->Just as the false form of the snake vanishes when the rope is known;<-
->So the false world vanishes when the knowledge of the Self is attained.<-

38. As, when the knowledge of the rope is obtained, the erroneous notion of its being a snake does not remain; so, by the arising of the knowledge of self, vanishes this universe based on illusion.

->Indeed, just as this silver-delusion vanishes upon the knowledge of the shell,<-
->So this world-delusion always vanishes through the knowledge of the Self.<-

39. As, when the knowledge of the mother-of-pearl is obtained, the erroneons notion of its being silver does not remain; so, through the knowledge of spirit, the world always appears a delusion.

->Just as a bamboo may appear to be a snake due to the application of a certain ointment,<-
->So this world appears as a delusion because of the pigment of habit and imagination.<-

40. As, when a man besmears his eyelids with the <term>collyrium</term> <note>A medicinal or magical eye-salve (anjana).</note> prepared from the fat of frogs, a bamboo appears like a serpent, so the world appears in the <term>Paramâtmâ</term>, owing to the delusive pigment of habit and imagination. <note>The "fat of frogs" refers to an ancient Indian folkloric belief or stage-magic trick where specific ointments were said to cause specific optical illusions.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the foundational non-dualist (Advaita) view that the universe is an illusion superimposed upon the one true Spirit. It employs famous analogies like the sun reflected in water and the rope mistaken for a snake to illustrate how the many arise from the One.</summary>

<keywords>Siva Samhita, Advaita Vedanta, Atman, Brahman, Maya, Superimposition, Pralaya, Universal Spirit, Consciousness, Metaphor of the Sun</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of reality and the Spirit, following the previous discussion on the origins of the universe and the nature of ignorance.</meta>

# CHAPTER I. 41-47.

---

->Just as the illusion of a snake disappears once the rope is recognized for what it is,<-
->And just as a white object only appears yellow through a defect of the eye and not otherwise,<-
->So too, through the defect of ignorance, the Spirit appears as the world—an illusion that is difficult to cast off.<-

**41.** Just as recognizing a rope causes the illusory snake to vanish, so does self-realization <note>Original: *Atma-jnana*; knowledge of the true Self or Spirit.</note> dissolve the illusion of the material world. Just as a person with jaundiced eyes sees white objects as yellow, the "disease" of ignorance makes the pure Spirit appear as this world. This is an error that is notoriously difficult to overcome.

->When the defect is cured, the patient perceives the white color correctly on their own;<-
->Similarly, when ignorance is destroyed through the knowledge of the Pure, the Spirit is revealed as it truly is.<-

**42.** When jaundice is cured, the patient naturally sees colors as they are. In the same way, when the veil of delusive ignorance is lifted, the true, unchanging nature of the Spirit is made manifest to the seeker.

->Just as a rope never actually becomes a snake in the past, present, or future,<-
->So the Spirit—which is beyond all qualities and remains stainless—never actually becomes the universe.<-

**43.** A rope never truly turns into a snake; it only appears that way. Whether in the past, present, or future, the rope’s essence remains unchanged. Likewise, the Spirit, which exists beyond the three *gunas* <note>The three fundamental qualities of nature: *sattva* (goodness/light), *rajas* (passion/activity), and *tamas* (darkness/inertia).</note> and is perfectly pure <note>Original: *Niranjana*; literally "without tint" or "unstained."</note>, never actually transforms into the physical universe.

->Those things that come and go are non-eternal; even the gods are subject to destruction.<-
->Some who are wise in the Scriptures have determined this through the realization of the Spirit.<-

**44.** Some wise sages, deeply learned in the sacred texts, have realized through their experience of the Spirit that even the *Devas* <note>The celestial beings or gods of the Hindu pantheon.</note>—including their king, Indra—are non-eternal. They are subject to the cycles of birth and death and will eventually face destruction.

->Just as foam and bubbles are produced in the ocean by the force of the wind,<-
->So this entire world, which is fleeting as a moment, arises within the Spirit.<-

**45.** Like a bubble rising in the sea due to a gust of wind, this transitory world <note>Original: *Samsara*; the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth.</note> arises from the Spirit. It is "moment-shattering"—existing only for a brief flash in the span of eternity.

->Unity is what truly shines forever; the division of things has no real existence.<-
->The distinctions of "two-fold" or "three-fold" are merely the end results of an illusion.<-

**46.** Unity <note>Original: *Abheda*; non-duality or the absence of difference.</note> is the eternal reality. The appearance of separate objects does not have a lasting existence. All concepts of duality, trinity, or manifold variety are ultimately products of a confused mind.

->Whatever has been, and whatever is yet to be, whether it has form or is formless—<-
->All of this universe is simply superimposed upon the Supreme Spirit.<-

**47.** Everything in time—past, present, and future—and everything in space, whether it has a physical shape or is an abstract concept, is merely "superimposed" <note>The philosophical concept of *vivarta*, where one thing appears as another without changing its own nature, like a movie projected onto a screen.</note> upon the Supreme Spirit.

<summary>This page uses classical metaphors—the rope and the snake, jaundiced eyes, and ocean bubbles—to explain that the material world is an illusory superimposition on the eternal, unchanging Spirit. It asserts that even the gods are temporary, while only the non-dual Unity of the Spirit is real.</summary>

<keywords>Atman, Spirit, Ignorance, Maya, Superimposition, Gunas, Devas, Indra, Samsara, Non-duality, Advaita Vedanta, Illusion</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of reality, shifting from the critique of false knowledge to the definition of the "Spirit" (Atman) or "Intelligence" (Chaitanya) as the only true reality.</meta>

SIVA SAMHITA.

---

->Ignorance is imagined by those who create mental constructs; it is born of falsehood and is inherently unreal.<-
->Since this world is rooted in such unreality, how could it ever be truly real? (48)<-

48. Suggested by the masters of mental construction comes <term>Avidyâ</term> <note>original: Avidyâ; commonly translated as "ignorance," it refers here to a fundamental misunderstanding of the nature of reality</note>. It is born of untruth, and its very essence is unreal. How can this world, built upon such foundations, be true?

## The Spirit.

->From Consciousness, this entire world of moving and unmoving things was born.<-
->Therefore, leaving everything else aside, one should take refuge in that Consciousness. (49)<-

49. All this universe, whether moving or stationary, has emerged from <term>Intelligence</term> <note>original: Chaitanya; the pure, conscious life-force of the universe</note>. Renouncing everything else, one should take shelter in that Intelligence alone.

->Just as space exists both inside and outside of a jar,<-
->so the Spirit exists eternally both within and beyond the world of created things. (50)<-

50. As <term>space</term> <note>original: Akasha; the ether or most subtle element that pervades all things</note> pervades a jar both inside and out, similarly, within and beyond this ever-changing universe, there exists one Universal Spirit.

->Just as space does not mix with the five false elements,<-
->so the Spirit does not mix with the world of created things; it cannot be otherwise. (51)<-

51. As the space pervading the five false states of matter does not mix with them, so the Spirit does not mix with this ever-changing universe.

->From the highest Gods down to the material world, all are pervaded by the Spirit.<-
->There is only one Existence-Consciousness-Bliss, complete and without a second. (52)<-

52. From the <term>Devas</term> <note>divine beings or gods</note> down to this material universe, all are pervaded by one Spirit. There is only one <term>Sachchidânanda</term> <note>A Sanskrit compound: Sat (Existence), Chit (Consciousness), and Ananda (Bliss)</note>, which is all-pervading and without a second.

->Because there is no other light to illuminate it, it must be self-luminous.<-
->Because it is self-luminous, the Spirit is, in its very nature, Light. (53)<-

53. Since it is not illuminated by anything else, it is self-luminous; and because of that self-luminosity, the very nature of the Spirit is Light.

->Because it is not limited by the nature of space or time,<-
->the Spirit is therefore truly infinite and complete. (54)<-

54. Since the Spirit in its nature is not limited by time or space, it is therefore infinite, all-pervading, and entirety itself.

->Since it cannot be destroyed by the five elements, which are themselves unreal,<-
->the Spirit is eternal and its destruction can never truly occur. (55)<-

55. Since the Spirit cannot be destroyed by the <term>five elements</term> <note>original: Panchabhuta; the traditional elements of Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Space/Ether</note>—which are themselves temporary and unreal—the Spirit is eternal and can never be destroyed.

<summary>This page argues that the material world is an unreal construct of ignorance, while the "Spirit" or "Intelligence" is the only eternal, self-luminous, and all-pervading reality.</summary>
<keywords>Avidya, Chaitanya, Intelligence, Spirit, Akasha, Sachchidananda, Devas, Panchabhuta, five elements, non-duality, self-luminosity</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of the Spirit (Atman) and its distinction from the material world, following the previous discussion on the unreality of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

# CHAPTER I. 55-62.

<sig>2</sig>

55. Since the <term>Spirit</term> <note>Sanskrit: Atman; the true, eternal self that is distinct from the physical body and mind.</note> is unlike this world, which is composed of five states of matter, that *are* false and subject to destruction, therefore, it *is* eternal. It is never destroyed.

> Since there is nothing else in this world other than That, it is forever One; since everything else besides That is false, the <term>Spirit</term> is truly Reality. <note>original Sanskrit: "यस्मात्तदन्यो नास्तीह..." (Verse 56)</note>

56. Save and beyond it, there is no other substance, therefore, it is one; without it everything else is false; therefore, it is <term>True Existence</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Sat; refers to absolute, unchanging reality.</note>.

> Because in this world created by <term>ignorance</term>, the destruction of sorrow is called happiness, and through <term>Gnosis</term> it becomes free from beginning and end, therefore the <term>Spirit</term> is <term>Bliss</term>. <note>original Sanskrit: "अविद्याभूतसंसारे..." (Verse 57)</note>

57. Since in this world created by <term>ignorance</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Avidya; a fundamental misunderstanding of the nature of reality that keeps the soul trapped in the cycle of rebirth.</note>, the destruction of sorrow means the gaining of happiness; and, through <term>Gnosis</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Jnana; higher spiritual knowledge or direct realization.</note>, immunity from all sorrow ensues; therefore, the <term>Spirit</term> is <term>Bliss</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Ananda; the state of pure, unconditional spiritual joy.</note>.

> Since <term>Gnosis</term> destroys <term>Ignorance</term>, which is the cause of the universe, the <term>Spirit</term> is therefore <term>Gnosis</term>, and this <term>Gnosis</term> is consequently eternal. <note>original Sanskrit: "यस्मान्नाशितमज्ञानं..." (Verse 58)</note>

58. Since by <term>Gnosis</term> is destroyed the <term>Ignorance</term>, which is the cause of the universe; therefore, the <term>Spirit</term> is <term>Gnosis</term>; and this <term>Gnosis</term> is consequently eternal.

> Since this manifold universe takes its origin in time, there is One who is verily the <term>Self</term>, unchanging through all ages, who is one and beyond the path of thought. <note>original Sanskrit: "कालतो विविधं विश्वं..." (Verse 59)</note>

59. Since in time this manifold universe takes its origin, therefore, there is One who is verily the <term>Self</term>, unchanging through all times. Who is one, and unthinkable.

> All these external things will perish in the course of time; but the <term>Spirit</term>, which is beyond the reach of words, remains without a second. <note>original Sanskrit: "बाह्यानि सर्वभूतानि..." (Verse 60)</note>

60. All these external substances will perish in the course of time; (but) that <term>Spirit</term> which is indescribable by word (will exist) without a second.

> Neither ether, air, fire, water, nor earth; neither their effects nor the <term>Devas</term> are perfect; the <term>Spirit</term> alone is truly complete. <note>original Sanskrit: "न खं वायुर्न चाग्निश्च..." (Verse 61)</note>

61. Neither ether, air, fire, water, earth, nor their combinations, nor the <term>Devas</term> <note>The Devas are the gods or celestial beings of Indian tradition; here, the text suggests even they are finite and subject to the laws of the universe, unlike the absolute Spirit.</note>, are perfect; the <term>Spirit</term> alone is so.

### Yoga and Māyâ.

> The <term>Yogi</term> certainly sees the Universal <term>Spirit</term> within his own self by the self, having renounced all worldly intentions and abandoned the grip of this false existence. <note>original Sanskrit: "आत्मानमात्मनो योगी..." (Verse 62)</note>

62. Having renounced all false desires and abandoned all false worldly chains, the <term>Yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of Yoga who seeks spiritual liberation through discipline and meditation.</note> sees certainly in his own spirit the Universal <term>Spirit</term> by the self.

<vocab>Spirit, True Existence, ignorance, Gnosis, Bliss, Ignorance, Self, Devas, Yoga, Māyâ, Yogi</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the Spirit (Atman) as the only eternal reality, characterized as Existence, Knowledge, and Bliss, while all material elements and even gods are described as finite. It concludes that a Yogi realizes this universal truth by detaching from worldly desires and looking inward.</summary>
<keywords>Atman, Sat-Chit-Ananda, Avidya, Jnana, Five Elements, Devas, Yoga, Liberation, Shiva Samhita</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of reality and the soul from previous sections of the Siva Samhita, specifically focusing on the transition from worldly illusion to spiritual liberation.</meta>

### SIVA SAMHITA

<page-num>10</page-num>

->Having perceived the eternal, blissful Self within the self,<-
->And forgetting the universe, he delights in the intensity of deep meditation.<-

63. Having seen the Spirit, that brings forth happiness, in his own spirit by the help of the self, he forgets this universe, and enjoys the ineffable bliss of *Samâdhi* <note>original: "Samâdhi"—a state of intense concentration or profound meditative absorption where the practitioner unites with the divine.</note> (profound meditation.)

->Illusion alone is the mother of the universe; through the eye of truth, there is no other.<-
->When this illusion is destroyed, the world truly ceases to exist.<-

64. *Mâyâ* <note>original: "Mâyâ"—often translated as "illusion," it refers to the powerful force that creates the perceptible universe, masking the true underlying reality.</note> (illusion) is the mother of the universe. Not from any other principle has the universe been created; when this *Mâyâ* is destroyed, the world certainly does not exist.

->Everything seen here is to be cast aside, as it is only the play of illusion.<-
->Thus, things like the body, wealth, and worldly happiness are no longer objects of affection.<-

65. He, to whom this world is but the pleasure-ground of *Mâyâ*, therefore, contemptible and worthless, cannot find any happiness in riches, body, etc., nor in pleasures.

->The world appears in three ways: as a friend, an enemy, or something indifferent.<-
->In worldly dealings, this is always observed and never otherwise;<-
->The distinction between what is pleasant and unpleasant in objects is fixed and clear.<-

66. This world appears in three different aspects to men—either friendly, inimical, or indifferent; such is always found in worldly dealings; there is distinction also in substances, as they are good, bad or indifferent.

->Only because of the soul’s limiting conditions do "sons" and others exist.<-
->Through scripture and logic, the universe is known to be the play of illusion.<-
->Yogis dissolve the world through superimposition and its removal.<-

67. That one Spirit, through differentiation, verily becomes a son, a father, etc. The Sacred Scriptures have demonstrated the universe to be the freak of *Mâyâ* (illusion). The Yogî <note>A practitioner of Yoga.</note> destroys this phenomenal universe by realising that it is but the result of <term>Adhyâropa</term> (superimposition: the act of falsely attributing the qualities of the material world to the pure Spirit) and by means of <term>Apavâda</term> (refutation: the logical process of stripping away these false attributes to reveal the truth).

->*Definition of a Parama Hansa.*<-
<note>A "Parama Hansa" (Supreme Swan) is a title given to a liberated soul who has achieved the highest level of spiritual grace and can distinguish the eternal from the ephemeral.</note>

->When a man is truly free from all limiting conditions,<-
->Then he is revealed as indivisible knowledge, pure and stainless.<-

68. When a person is free from the infinite distinctions and states of existence as caste, individuality etc., then he can say that he is indivisible intelligence, and pure Unit.

<summary>This page describes how a practitioner (Yogî) achieves liberation by recognizing that the world is an illusion (Mâyâ) and transcending worldly relationships and perceptions to realize their true identity as pure, indivisible consciousness.</summary>

<keywords>Samâdhi, Mâyâ, Adhyâropa, Apavâda, Parama Hansa, Yogî, Siva Samhita, illusion, superimposition, spiritual liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the origins of the universe, moving from the nature of the Absolute to the specific sequence of elemental creation.</meta>

### *Emanation or Evolution.*

->That Supreme Person willed: "I shall create offspring." From that will, Ignorance appears; therefore, the universe is by its very nature illusory.<- <note>original Sanskrit: सो कामयतः पुरुषः सृजते च प्रजाः स्वयम् । अविद्या भासते यस्मात्तस्मान्मिथ्या स्वभावतः ॥ ६९ ॥</note>

69. The Lord willed to create his creatures; from His will came out <term>Avidyâ</term> (Ignorance), the mother of this false universe. <note>In this context, Ignorance is not a lack of facts, but the fundamental illusion that makes the one appear as many.</note>

->A connection occurs between the Pure Absolute and Ignorance. From this union, the Creator arises, and from Him, the Ether is manifested.<- <note>original Sanskrit: शुद्ध ब्रह्मत्व संबदो विद्यया सहितो भवेत् । ब्रह्मतेनसती याति यत आभासते नभः ॥ ७० ॥</note>

70. There <unclear>takes</unclear> place the conjunction between the Pure <term>Brahma</term> <note>The impersonal, infinite Absolute</note> and <term>Avidyâ</term>, from which arises <term>Brahmâ</term> <note>The personified Creator deity</note>, from which comes out the <term>Akâśa</term> <note>The element of space or ether</note>.

->From Ether, the Air is manifested; from Air, Fire; from Fire, Water. From Water, the Earth is manifested. This is how the subtle evolution is established.<- <note>original Sanskrit: तस्मात्प्रकाशते वायुर्वायोरग्निस्ततो जलम् । प्रकाशते ततः पृथ्वी कल्पनेय' स्थिता सति ॥ ७१ ॥</note>

71. From the <term>Akâśa</term> emanated the air; from air came the fire; from fire—water; and from water came the earth. This is the order of subtle emanation.

->From ether comes air; from the combination of ether and air, fire is born. From the mixture of ether, air, and fire comes water; and from the union of ether, air, fire, and water, the earth is produced.<- <note>original Sanskrit: आकाशाद्वायुराकाशपवनादग्निसंभवः । खवाताग्नेर्जलं व्योमवाताग्नेरिवारितो मही ॥ ७२ ॥</note>

72. From ether, air; from the air and ether combined came fire; from the triple compound of ether, air and fire came water; and from the combination of ether, air, fire and water was produced the (gross) earth. <note>This describes a process called "triplication" or "quintuplication," where elements combine to become solid matter.</note>

->The quality of ether is sound; air is characterized by motion and touch. Fire is known by its form, and water by its taste. The earth is characterized by smell; it is certainly not otherwise.<- <note>original Sanskrit: खं शब्दलक्षणं वायुश्च'लः स्पर्शलक्षणः । स्याद्रूपलक्षणं तेजः सलिलं रसलक्षणम् ॥ गन्धलक्षणिका पृथ्वी नान्यथा भवति ध्रुवम् ॥ ७३ ॥</note>

73. The quality of ether is sound; of air motion and touch. Form is the quality of fire, and taste of water. And smell is the quality of the earth. There is no gainsaying this.

->Ether has one quality; air is said to have two. Likewise, fire has three qualities, and water has four. Sound, touch, form, taste, and smell—these five qualities the wise now assign to the earth.<- <note>original Sanskrit: स्यादेकगुणमाकाशं द्विगुणो वायुरुच्यते । तथैव त्रिगुणं तेजो भवन्त्यापश्चतुर्गुणाः ॥ शब्दः स्पर्शश्च रूपं च रसो गन्धस्तथैव च । एततपंचगुणा पृथ्वी कल्पकैः कल्प्यतेऽधुना ॥ ७४ ॥</note>

74. <term>Akâśa</term> has one quality; air two, fire three, water four, and earth five qualities, *viz,*—sound, touch, taste, form and smell. This has been declared by the wise.

->Form is perceived by the eye, and smell is perceived by the nose. Taste is perceived by the tongue, while touch is felt through the skin.<- <note>original Sanskrit: चक्षुषा गृह्यते रूपं गन्धो घ्राणेन गृह्यते । रसो रसनया स्पर्शस्त्वचा संगृह्यते परम् ॥ ७५ ॥</note>

->Sound is perceived by the ear; this is a fixed law and does not appear otherwise.<- <note>original Sanskrit: श्रोत्रेण गृह्यते शब्दो नियतं भाति नान्यथा ॥ ७६ ॥</note>

<summary>This page details the Vedic cosmological process where the Supreme Being uses Ignorance (Avidya) to manifest the five elements—Ether, Air, Fire, Water, and Earth—and their corresponding sensory qualities.</summary>
<keywords>Emanation, Evolution, Avidya, Akasha, Brahma, Brahma, Five Elements, Sensory Qualities, Sound, Touch, Taste, Form, Smell</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of reality and the senses from page 11. It transitions from the mechanisms of perception to the process of cosmological dissolution and the role of the creative illusion.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA

---

75-76. Form is perceived through the eyes, smell through the nose, taste through the tongue, touch through the skin, and sound through the ear. These are truly the organs of perception.

<note>original: "चैतन्यात्सर्वमुत्पन्नं जगदेतच्चराचरम् । अस्ति चेत्कल्पनेयं स्यान्नास्ति चेदस्ति चिन्मयम् ॥ ७७ ॥"</note>
77. From <term>Pure Consciousness</term><note>Sanskrit: Chaitanya; the ultimate, formless awareness.</note> has emerged this entire universe, both moving and unmoving. If it is perceived to exist, it is a mental construction; if it is perceived not to exist, then only the "All-Conscious" One remains.

### *Absorption or Involution*

<note>original: "पृथ्वी शीर्णा जले मग्ना जलं मग्नञ्च तेजसि । लीनं वायौ तथा तेजो व्योम्नि वातो लयं ययौ ॥ अविद्यायां महाकाशो लीयते परमे पदे ॥ ७८ ॥"</note>
78. The element of earth becomes subtle and dissolves into water; water is resolved into fire; fire similarly merges into air; air is absorbed into the <term>ether</term><note>Sanskrit: Akasha; the space or vacuum through which sound travels.</note>; and the ether is resolved into <term>Ignorance</term><note>Sanskrit: Avidya; a lack of spiritual knowledge that creates the illusion of the physical world.</note>, which finally merges into the <term>Great Absolute</term><note>Sanskrit: Brahma; the supreme reality.</note>.

<note>original: "विक्षेपावरणा शक्तिर्दुर्न्तासुखरूपिणी ॥ जडरूपा महामाया रजःसत्त्वतमोगुणा ॥ ७९ ॥"</note>
79. There are two forces—<term>projection</term><note>Sanskrit: vikshepa; the energy that causes the world to appear.</note> and <term>concealment</term><note>Sanskrit: avarana; the energy that hides the true reality.</note>—which possess great potential and power, and whose inherent form is bliss. The great <term>Illusion</term><note>Sanskrit: Maya; the power that produces the manifest world.</note>, when considered in its material and non-intelligent form, is composed of three attributes: <term>balance</term><note>Sanskrit: sattva.</note>, <term>energy</term><note>Sanskrit: rajas.</note>, and <term>inertia</term><note>Sanskrit: tamas.</note>.

<note>original: "सा मायावरणशक्त्यावृताविज्ञानरूपिणी ॥ दर्शयेज्जगदाकारं तं विक्षेपस्वभावतः ॥ ८० ॥"</note>
80. The material form of <term>Illusion</term>, when veiled by the force of <term>concealment</term>, manifests itself as the physical universe through the natural action of the <term>projective</term> force.

<note>original: "तमो गुणाधिका विद्या या सा दुर्गा भवेत् स्वयम्... ॥ ८१ ॥"</note>
81. When <term>Ignorance</term> is dominated by the quality of <term>inertia</term><note>Sanskrit: tamas.</note>, it manifests as the goddess Durga; the consciousness that presides over this form is known as the <term>Supreme Lord</term><note>Sanskrit: Isvara.</note>.

81 (a). When <term>Ignorance</term> is dominated by the quality of <term>balance</term><note>Sanskrit: sattva.</note>, it manifests as the beautiful goddess Lakshmi; the Consciousness that presides over this form is called Vishnu.

<summary>This page describes the sensory organs and the process of cosmological dissolution, where physical elements return to their source in Pure Consciousness. It also introduces the two powers of Illusion (projection and concealment) and explains how different divine forms emerge from the three fundamental qualities of nature.</summary>

<keywords>perception, Pure Consciousness, Involution, elements, Maya, Gunas, Durga, Lakshmi, Vishnu, dissolution of the universe</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of reality, specifically describing how the Supreme Spirit manifests as various deities and the material world through the lens of primordial ignorance and the qualities of nature.</meta>

# CHAPTER I. 82-88.

->The wisdom characterized by a predominance of the quality of activity is known as Saraswati;<-
->The consciousness that supports her is known as the creator, Brahma. || 82 ||<-

82. When ignorance (<term>avidyâ</term>—the veil that hides reality) has an excess of the quality of passion or movement (<term>rajus</term>), it manifests itself as the wise goddess <term>Saraswati</term> <note>Saraswati is the patron of arts and science; here she represents the active power of knowledge</note>; the intelligence which presides over her is known as the creator god <term>Brahmâ</term>.

->All gods, beginning with Shiva, are seen within the Supreme Self;<-
->The body and all inert matter are the various forms of that ignorance. || 83 ||<-

83. Gods like <term>Śiva</term> <note>the Destroyer</note>, <term>Brahmâ</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term> <note>the Preserver</note>, etc., are all seen in the great Spirit; bodies and all material objects are the various products of <term>avidyâ</term> <note>ignorance or the creative illusion</note>.

->In this way, the creators conceive the origin of the universe;<-
->Therefore, the elements and non-elements are produced here by this conception. || 84 ||<-

84. The wise have thus explained the creation of the world—the <term>tattwas</term> (the fundamental elements or principles of reality) and the non-elements are thus produced—and not in any other way.

->All objects are revealed through the quality of being knowable;<-
->Distinction arises only through the use of specific names, not otherwise. || 85 ||<-

85. All things are seen as finite and measurable <note>original: "prameyatvâdirûpeṇa," meaning having the nature of an object of knowledge</note>, and there arise various distinctions merely through words and names; but there is no real difference.

->Likewise, the objects themselves do not exist; only the supreme Witness exists;<-
->An object is spoken of as having its own form only because it reflects the true Essence. || 86 ||<-

86. Therefore, the things do not exist; the great and glorious One that manifests them, alone exists; though things are false and unreal, yet, as the reflection of the real, they, for the time being, appear real.

->The One, filled with the nature of existence and bliss, complete and all-pervading, exists; nothing else does.<-
->He who constantly possesses this knowledge is freed from death and the sorrows of the world-wheel. || 87 ||<-

87. The One Entity, blissful, entire and all-pervading, alone exists, and nothing else; he who constantly realizes this knowledge is freed from death and the sorrow of the <term>world-wheel</term> <note>original: "saṃsāra," the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth</note>.

->When everything is dissolved into the One through superimposition and refutation;<-
->That One alone remains, and this is grasped by the mind. || 88 ||<-

88. When, through the knowledge that all is illusory perception (<term>âropa</term>—superimposing qualities onto the Absolute) and by intellectual refutation (<term>apavâda</term>—the systematic denial of those false qualities) of other doctrines, this universe is resolved into the One, then there exists only that One and nothing else; then this is clearly perceived by the mind.

<summary>This page describes how the universe and its deities are manifestations of a single underlying Reality filtered through qualities like 'activity.' It emphasizes that the material world is a mental construct or name-based distinction, and liberation comes from realizing the Unity of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Saraswati, Brahma, Rajas (Activity), Avidya (Ignorance), Tattwas (Principles), Samsara (World-wheel), Aropa (Superimposition), Apavada (Refutation), Non-duality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of existence, shifting focus from the origins of the universe to the formation of the individual physical body and the role of Karma.</meta>

# SHIVA SAMHITA

<page-num>14</page-num>

->*Action (Karma) clothes the individual soul (Jiva) with a body.*<-

**Born from the father's physical sheath and driven by past actions, this body is known by the wise as a vessel of suffering, designed for the soul to experience its previous deeds.**

89. From the <term>Annamaya Kośa</term> <note>The "food-made" sheath, referring to the physical body sustained by nourishment</note> of the father, and in accordance with its past <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect based on previous actions</note>, the human soul is reincarnated. Therefore, those who possess wisdom view even a beautiful body as a form of "punishment"—a necessary tool for enduring the consequences of past lives.

**This temple of experience is built of flesh, bone, sinew, and marrow; it is a tangled web of vessels meant only for the enduring of sorrow.**

90. This body is a "temple" where both pleasure and pain are felt. It is constructed from physical matter—flesh, bones, nerves, marrow, and blood—and is intricately woven with blood vessels and energy channels. Its primary purpose in this cycle of existence is the processing of past suffering.

**This celestial frame is composed of the five elements; it is known as the "Microcosm," fashioned for the experience of both joy and pain.**

91. This body, the dwelling place of the supreme spirit (<term>Brahma</term>), is made from the five great elements <note>Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether</note>. It is called the <term>Brahmâṇḍa</term> <note>Literally "the Egg of Brahma," meaning the microcosm or the universe in miniature</note>. It has been specifically designed to allow the soul to feel the full range of worldly pleasure and pain.

**From the union of the Seed and the Energy, all creatures are born through their own inherent, material power.**

92. All living beings arise from the spontaneous combination of the two primordial principles: <term>Śiva</term> <note>Pure consciousness or the Spirit</note> and <term>Śakti</term> <note>Creative energy or Matter</note>. Through the interaction of these forces, the material world is brought into being.

**Through the five-fold combination of elements, countless gross objects are formed in this universe. The consciousness bound within them by its actions is called the individual soul.**

93. Through a process called <term>Panchikarana</term> <note>The mixing of the five subtle elements to create physical matter</note>, the vast variety of physical objects in the universe is produced. The intelligence or spirit that becomes confined within these material forms due to its past actions is called the <term>Jîva</term> <note>The individual soul or living being</note>. This entire world is derived from the five elements, and the soul resides within it to reap what it has sown.

**"According to the requirements of past actions, I arrange their destinies. The soul is immaterial and exists in all things, yet it experiences the world through the material body."**

94. <note>Here, the Divine voice speaks</note> In strict alignment with the effects of a soul's past <term>Karma</term>, I regulate its destiny. Though the <term>Jîva</term> is itself non-material and present everywhere, it enters a physical, "dull" body to encounter and "consume" the fruits of its previous actions.

<summary>This page explains the yogic view of embryology and incarnation, describing the physical body as a "microcosm" (Brahmanda) made of five elements, created specifically to allow the soul to resolve its past Karma.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Jiva, Annamaya Kośa, Brahmâṇḍa, Shiva, Shakti, Microcosm, Five Elements, Reincarnation, Panchikarana</keywords>

# Chapter I. 95-96.

---

->**Bound by their own actions to inert matter, the individual souls take on various forms.**<-
->**For the sake of experience, they are born again and again into this universe.**<-
->**[Verse 95]**<-

95. Bound in the chain of matter by their <term>actions <note>Sanskrit: karma. In this context, it refers to the law of cause and effect where every deed influences future existence.</note></term>, the <term>individual souls <note>Sanskrit: Jivas. The Jiva is the spark of life or individual consciousness that inhabits a physical body.</note></term> receive various names and forms. In this <term>universe <note>Sanskrit: Brahmanda, literally "The Egg of Brahma," referring to the entire created cosmos.</note></term>, they return time and again to undergo the consequences of their previous deeds.

->**The soul is dissolved when the experience of its own actions reaches its end.**<-
->**[Verse 96]**<-

96. When the fruits of one's <term>actions <note>Sanskrit: karma</note></term> have been fully experienced and exhausted, the <term>individual soul <note>Sanskrit: Jiva</note></term> is absorbed back into the <term>Supreme Absolute <note>Sanskrit: Parambrahma. The ultimate, formless reality or Godhead in Hindu philosophy.</note></term>.

# CHAPTER II.

## (1). *The microcosm.*

->In this body exists Mount <term>Meru</term><note>In Hindu cosmology, Meru is the sacred mountain at the center of the universe. In Yoga, it refers to the vertebral column or spine as the body's central axis.</note>, surrounded by the seven islands; there are rivers, oceans, mountains, fields, and the guardians of these fields. ॥ 1 ॥<-

In this body, the mount <term>Meru</term>—that is, the vertebral column—is surrounded by seven islands; there are rivers, seas, mountains, fields; and lords of the fields too.

->There are in it seers and sages; all the stars and planets as well. There are sacred pilgrimages, shrines; and the presiding deities of those shrines. ॥ 2 ॥<-

2. There are in it seers and sages; all the stars and planets as well. There are sacred pilgrimages, shrines; and presiding deities of the shrines.

->The sun and moon, the agents of creation and destruction, also move within it. Ether, air, fire, water, and earth are also present. ॥ 3 ॥<-

3. The sun and moon, agents of creation and destruction, also move in it. Ether, air, fire, water and earth are also there.

## (2). *The Nerve Centres.*

->Whatever beings exist in the three worlds are all to be found within the body; surrounding the <term>Meru</term>, they are engaged in their respective functions. ॥ 4 ॥<-

4. All the beings that exist in the three worlds are also to be found in the body; surrounding the <term>Meru</term> they are engaged in their respective functions.

->He who knows all of this is a <term>Yogî</term><note>A practitioner of Yoga who has achieved a high state of spiritual realization.</note>; of this, there is no doubt. ॥ 5 ॥<-

5. (But ordinary men do not know it). He who knows all this is a <term>Yogî</term>; there is no doubt about it.

->In this body, which is known as the <term>Brahmâṇḍa</term><note>Literally "the egg of Brahma," representing the entire universe or microcosm.</note>, there is the nectar-rayed moon situated in its proper place at the top of the spinal cord, possessing eight <term>Kalâs</term><note>Digits or phases of the moon; here referring to specific energy divisions.</note> in the shape of a semi-circle. ॥ 6 ॥<-

6. In this body, which is called <term>Brahmâṇḍa</term> (microcosm, literally the mundane egg), there is the nectar-rayed moon, in its proper place, on the top of the spinal cord, with eight <term>Kalâs</term> (in the shape of a semi-circle).

->This moon remains there day and night, raining nectar downwards. From that, the ambrosia sub-divides into two subtle parts: ॥ 7 ॥<-

7. This has its face downwards, and rains nectar day and night. The ambrosia further sub-divides itself into two subtle parts:

### CHAPTER II. 8-15.

<note>original: "इडामाग्रेण पुष्ट्यर्थं याति मन्दाकिनीजलम् । पुष्पाति सकलं देहमिडामाग्रेण निश्चितम् ॥ ८॥"</note>
8. Following the path of the <term>Idā</term> <note>the energy channel associated with the moon and the left side of the body</note> for the sake of nourishment, the waters of the heavenly Ganges flow. This nectar-like fluid certainly nourishes the entire body through the <term>Idā</term> channel.

<note>original: "एष पीयूषरश्मिर्हि वामपार्श्वे व्यवस्थितः ॥ अपरः शुद्धदुग्धाभो हठात्कर्षति मण्डलात् । मध्यमार्गेण सृष्ट्यर्थं मेरौ संयाति चन्द्रमाः ॥ ९ ॥"</note>
9. This nectar-rayed moon is situated on the left side. Another ray, as brilliant as pure milk and a source of great joy, is drawn forcefully from the lunar sphere; for the sake of creation, the moon enters the <term>Meru</term> <note>the spinal column, named after the sacred mountain at the center of the universe</note> through the middle path known as the <term>Sushumnā</term>.

<note>original: "मेरुमूले स्थितः सूर्यः कलाद्वादशसंयुतः । दक्षिणे पथि रश्मिभिर्वहत्यूर्ध्वं प्रजापतिः ॥ १० ॥"</note>
10. At the base of the <term>Meru</term> sits the sun, possessed of twelve <term>Kalās</term> <note>phases or specific powers of solar energy</note>. In the right-side path, known as the <term>Pingalā</term>, the Lord of Creatures <note>the Sun</note> carries the vital fluids upward through his rays.

<note>original: "पीयूषरश्मिनिर्यासं धातूंश्च ग्रसति ध्रुवम् । समीरमण्डले सूर्यो भ्रमते सर्वविग्रहे ॥ ११ ॥"</note>
11. This sun certainly consumes the nectar-like secretions and the essential bodily elements. Together with the atmosphere or breath, the sun moves throughout the entire body.

<note>original: "एषा सूर्यपरामूर्तिः निर्वाणं दक्षिणे पथि । वहते लग्नयोगेन सृष्टिसंहारकारकः ॥ १२ ॥"</note>
12. The right-side vessel, the <term>Pingalā</term>, is another form of the sun and is the giver of <term>Nirvāna</term> <note>spiritual liberation</note>. The sun, who acts as the author of both creation and destruction, moves in this vessel in harmony with the auspicious signs of the zodiac.

---

### (3).---The Nerves.

<note>original: "सार्धलक्षत्रयं नाड्यः सन्ति देहान्तरे नृणाम् । प्रधानभूता नाड्यस्तु तासु मुख्याश्चतुर्दशः ॥ १३ ॥"</note>
13. Within the human body, there are 350,000 <term>nādis</term> <note>subtle energy channels or nerves</note>; of these, fourteen are considered the principal ones.

<note>original: "सुषुम्णेडा पिंगला च गांधारी हस्तिजिह्विका । कुहूः सरस्वती पूषा शंखिनी च पयस्वनी ॥ १४ ॥ वारुण्यलम्बुसा चैव विश्वोदरी यशस्विनी । एतासु तिस्रो मुख्याः स्युः पिंगलेडा सुषुम्णिका ॥ १५ ॥"</note>
14-15. These are the <term>Sushumnā</term>, <term>Idā</term>, <term>Pingalā</term>, <term>Gāndhārī</term>, <term>Hastijihvikā</term>, <term>Kuhū</term>, <term>Saraswatī</term>, <term>Pūshā</term>, <term>Shankhinī</term>, <term>Payaswanī</term>, <term>Vārunī</term>, <term>Alumbushā</term>, <term>Vishwodharī</term>, and <term>Yashaswinī</term>. Among these, the <term>Idā</term>, <term>Pingalā</term>, and <term>Sushumnā</term> are the most important.

# SIVA SAMHITA.

->Among these three, the <term>Sushumnâ</term> <note>The central energy channel that runs along the spine in the subtle body</note> alone is the most important and is beloved by the Yogis. The other energy channels in the bodies of living beings are dependent upon it. ॥ 16 ॥<-

16. Among these three, <term>Sushumnâ</term> alone is the highest and beloved of the Yogis. Other vessels are subordinate to it in the body.

->All these energy channels face downwards and are as delicate as the fine threads of a lotus. They are supported by the spinal column and embody the nature of the moon, the sun, and fire. ॥ 17 ॥<-

17. All these principal <term>nâdis</term> <note>vessels or channels through which life force flows</note> have their mouths downwards, and are like thin threads of lotus. They are all supported by the vertebral column, and represent the sun, moon and fire.

->Inside them resides the channel called <term>Chitrâ</term>, which is very dear to me. Within it is the <term>Brahmaraudhra</term>, the most auspicious and subtlest of all openings. ॥ 18 ॥<-

18. The innermost of these three is *<term>Chitrâ</term>* <note>The "luminous" or "bright" channel within the Sushumna</note>; it is my beloved. In that there is the subtlest of all hollows called <term>Brahmaraudhra</term> <note>original: "Brahmaraudhra," likely referring to the Brahmarandhra or "Aperture of Brahman" at the crown of the head</note>.

->Brilliant with five colors, pure, and moving through the center of the <term>Sushumnâ</term>, this <term>Chitrâ</term> is the vital essence of the body and the heart of the <term>Sushumnâ</term>. ॥ 19 ॥<-

19. Brilliant with five colours, pure, moving in the middle of *<term>Sushumnâ</term>*, this *<term>Chitrâ</term>* is the vital part of body and centre of *<term>Sushumnâ</term>*.

->This has been called the Divine Path in the <term>Sâstras</term>, the source of the bliss of immortality. By merely meditating upon it, the great <term>Yogî</term> destroys the entire mass of their sins. ॥ 20 ॥<-

20. This has been called in the *<term>Sâstras</term>* <note>authoritative sacred scriptures</note> the Heavenly Way; this is the giver of the joy of immortality; by contemplating it, the great *<term>Yogî</term>* <note>an advanced practitioner of Yoga</note> destroys all sins.

->(4).—The Pelvic Region.<-

->Two finger-widths above the anus and two finger-widths below the sexual organ, there exists the even-sided <term>âdhâra</term> lotus, measuring four finger-widths in extent. ॥ 21 ॥<-

21. Two digits above the rectum and two digits below the organ is the *<term>âdhâra</term>* <note>The "Base" or Muladhara chakra</note> lotus, having a dimension of four digits.

->In the center of that <term>âdhâra</term> lotus is a beautiful, triangular <term>yoni</term>, which is kept secret in all the <term>Tantras</term>. ॥ 22 ॥<-

22. In the pericarp <note>the seed-vessel or center</note> of the *<term>âdhâra</term>* lotus there is the triangular, beautiful *<term>yoni</term>* <note>a sacred space representing the source of creation</note>, hidden and kept secret in all the <term>Tantras</term> <note>esoteric spiritual texts</note>.

->Within it dwells the supreme goddess <term>Kundalini</term>, appearing like a flash of lightning. She is coiled three and a half times and rests at the entrance of the <term>Sushumnâ</term> path. ॥ 23 ॥<-

23. In it is the supreme goddess *<term>Kundalini</term>* <note>the dormant spiritual energy often visualized as a coiled serpent</note> of the form of electricity, in a coil. It has three coils and a half (like a serpent), and is in the mouth of *<term>Sushumnâ</term>*.

<header>CHAPTER II. 24-30. 19</header>

### CHAPTER II. 24-30.

->She is the embodiment of the world’s creation, eternally active in the work of building.<-
->She is the Goddess of Speech, whom words cannot fully express, ever honored by the divine beings. 24.<-

> 24. This creative power represents the foundational force of the universe and is perpetually engaged in the act of manifestation. She is the <term>Goddess of Speech</term><note>Sanskrit: *Vagdevi*, often identified with Saraswati.</note>, a reality that spoken language is unable to fully capture, and she receives the eternal adoration of all the gods.

->The energy channel named the Left-Side is situated on the left path.<-
->Coiling around the Central Channel, it proceeds to the right nostril. 25.<-

> 25. The <term>energy channel</term><note>Sanskrit: *nadi*—the pathways through which vital energy or *prana* flows.</note> known as the <term>Left-Side Channel</term><note>Sanskrit: *Ida*.</note> is located on the left side of the body. It wraps around the <term>Central Channel</term><note>Sanskrit: *Sushumna*.</note> and terminates at the right nostril.

->The energy channel named the Right-Side is situated on the right path.<-
->Coiling around the central vessel, it enters the left nostril. 26.<-

> 26. The <term>energy channel</term> known as the <term>Right-Side Channel</term><note>Sanskrit: *Pingala*.</note> is located on the right side; coiling around the central vessel, it travels to the left nostril.

->Between the Left and Right channels, the Central Channel truly resides.<-
->In six stages, the practitioners of yoga recognize six forces and six lotuses. 27.<-

> 27. The <term>energy channel</term> that exists between the Left and Right channels is indeed the <term>Central Channel</term>. Within it, <term>yogis</term><note>Sanskrit: *Yogin*—practitioners of spiritual disciplines.</note> recognize six stages, six vital forces, and six <term>lotuses</term><note>Sanskrit: *Padma*—referring to the *chakras* or energy centers located along the spine.</note>.

->The Central Channel has five regions and many different names.<-
->For practical purposes, these should be understood here through the sacred teachings. 28.<-

> 28. The first five stages of the <term>Central Channel</term> are known by various names; because they are essential for practice, they are explained in this <term>sacred text</term><note>Sanskrit: *Shastra*.</note>.

->Other secondary channels arise from the Root Center.<-
->They go to the tongue, the organs of generation, the eyes, the feet, the toes, and the ears.<-
->The belly, the armpit, the fingers, the ears, the whole body, the anus, and the abdomen.<-
->Having reached these places, they stop at the destinations where they emerged. 29.<-

> 29. Other <term>energy channels</term> emerge from the <term>Root Center</term><note>Sanskrit: *Muladhara*—the energy center at the base of the spine.</note> and travel to various parts of the body, including the tongue, the reproductive organs, the eyes, the feet, the toes, the ears, the abdomen, the armpits, the fingers, the scrotum, and the anus. After emerging from their place of origin, they conclude at these respective locations as described.

->From these primary channels, through branches and sub-branches in sequence,<-
->Three hundred and fifty thousand are born, distributed according to their parts. 30.<-

> 30. From these (fourteen) primary <term>energy channels</term>, a network of branches and sub-branches gradually develops, eventually numbering three hundred and fifty thousand, each supplying energy to its respective part of the body.

<note>The "five regions" or "Tantric stages" correspond to the anatomical divisions of the spinal column: the Cervical (neck), Dorsal (upper back), Lumbar (lower back), Sacral (pelvis), and Coccygeal (tailbone) regions.</note>

# SHIVA SAMHITA

->These sensation-bearing channels are skilled in the movement of the vital breath; they are interwoven and thoroughly pervade this physical frame.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "etā bhogavahā nāḍyo vāyusañcāradakṣakāḥ | otaprotāḥ susaṃvyāpya tiṣṭhantyasminkalevare"</note>

31. These <term>nadis</term> <note>The network of channels through which energy or prana flows in the body.</note> are spread through the body cross-wise and length-wise; they are vehicles of sensation and keep watch over the movements of the air—that is, they regulate the motor functions also.

### (5).—*The Abdominal Region.*

->Situated in the center of the solar sphere, consisting of twelve phases, the digestive fire burns in the region of the abdomen.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "sūryamaṇḍalamadhyasthaḥ kalādvādaśasaṃyutaḥ | vastideśe jvaladvahnirvartate cānnapācakaḥ"</note>
->This is the Fire of the Universal Man, born from a portion of my own brilliance; residing within the bodies of living beings, it performs the various types of digestion.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "eṣa vaiśvānaro’gnirvai mama tejoṃśasambhavaḥ | karoti vividhaṃ pākaṃ prāṇināṃ dehamāśritaḥ"</note>

32. In the abdomen there burns the fire—digestor of food—situated in the middle of the sphere of the sun having twelve <term>Kalas</term> <note>Literally "parts" or "phases," often used to describe the divisions of the sun or moon's power.</note>. Know this as the fire of <term>Vaishvanara</term> <note>The "Universal Man" fire, personified as the heat of digestion.</note>; it is born from a portion of my own energy, and digests the various foods of creatures, being inside their bodies.

->This fire is the giver of long life, strength, and nourishment; it provides bodily dexterity and destroys the very source of diseases.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "āyuḥ pradāyako vahnirbalaṃ puṣṭiṃ dadāti saḥ | śarīrapāṭavañcāpi dhvastarogasamudbhavaḥ"</note>

33. This fire increases life, and gives strength and nourishment, makes the body full of energy, destroys all diseases, and gives health.

->Therefore, the wise practitioner, having properly kindled this fire of the Universal Man, should daily offer food into it as a sacrifice, following the teacher's instructions.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "tasmādvaiśvānara-agniśca prajvālya vidhivatsudhīḥ | tasminnannaṃ huvedyogī pratyahaṃ guruśikṣayā"</note>

34. The wise <term>Yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga.</note>, having kindled this fire of the Universal Man according to proper rites, should sacrifice food into it every day, in conformity with the teachings of his spiritual teacher.

->In this body, known as the "Egg of Brahma" (the microcosm), there are many locations; I have described the principal ones that must be known in this scripture.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "brahmāṇḍasaṃjñake dehe sthānāni syurbahūni ca | mayoktāni pradhānāni jñātavyānīha śāstrake"</note>

35. This body called the <term>Brahmanda</term> <note>The "Egg of Brahma," meaning the microcosm of the universe contained within the human form.</note> has many parts, but I have enumerated the most important of them in this book. (Surely) they ought to be known.

->There are various names and diverse locations within this physical form; all of them cannot possibly be described.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "nānāprakāranāmāni sthānāni vividhāni ca | vartante vigrahe tāni kathituṃ naiva śakyate"</note>

36. Various are their names, and innumerable are the places in this human body; all of them cannot be enumerated here.

### (16). --*The Individual Soul.* <note>original title: "The Jivātmâ"</note>

->In this body thus fashioned, the individual soul resides, all-pervading; it is adorned with the garland of beginningless latent desires and bound by the chains of action.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "itthaṃ prakalpite dehe jīvo vasati sarvagaḥ | anādivāsanāmālā’laṅkṛtaḥ karmaśṛṅkhalaḥ"</note>

37. Within the body constructed in this way, the individual soul <note>The <term>Jiva</term> or spark of consciousness.</note> dwells, pervading everything. It is decorated by a never-ending cycle of past desires and is held fast by the chains of <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect resulting from one's actions.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the network of sensory channels (nadis) and the digestive fire (Vaishvanara) as a sacred internal sacrifice, concluding with the introduction of the individual soul (Jivatma) as a bound inhabitant of the body.</summary>
<keywords>nadis, Vaishvanara, digestion as sacrifice, Brahmanda, microcosm, Jivatma, karma, spiritual physiology</keywords>
