<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the human body and the soul's entanglement within it, following the anatomical and esoteric descriptions of the previous sections.</meta>

### CHAPTER II. 37-43.

37. In the body thus described, there dwells the <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jîva"</note>, all-pervading, adorned with the garland of endless desires and chained to the body by the results of past <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karma"</note>.

->Possessed of many qualities and the agent of all activities,<-
->it enjoys various results of actions amassed in the past life. 38.<-

38. The <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jîva"</note>, possessed of many qualities and the actor behind all events, enjoys the fruits of its various <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karmas"</note> gathered in previous existences.

->Whatever is seen in the world is born of action.<-
->All creatures enjoy or suffer according to the results of their deeds. 39.<-

39. Whatever is perceived among humanity—whether it be pleasure or pain—is born of <term>action</term> <note>original: "karma"</note>. All living beings experience life’s rewards or punishments strictly according to the consequences of their previous deeds.

->Those faults such as desire, which provide pleasure and pain,<-
->all proceed according to the actions of the Individual Soul. 40.<-

40. Desires and other such impulses, which bring about pleasure or pain, function according to the past <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karma"</note> of the <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jîva"</note>.

->When consciousness is colored by merit, it simply pleases the life-breaths.<-
->In the world, objects of enjoyment attained through merit appear spontaneously. 41.<-

41. The <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jîva"</note> that has accumulated an abundance of <term>virtuous deeds</term> <note>original: "punya"</note> receives a happy life; and in the world, it obtains pleasant and good things to enjoy without effort.

->From the power of action, man receives pleasure or pain.<-
->Consciousness colored by sin certainly does not remain at peace.<-
->It is not separate from that, nor is anything else separate.<-
->From consciousness veiled by Illusion, all things are produced. 42.<-

42. In proportion to the force of his <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karma"</note>, a person suffers misery or enjoys pleasure. The <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jîva"</note> that has accumulated an excess of <term>sinful deeds</term> <note>original: "papa"</note> never finds peace—it is not separate from its actions; indeed, nothing exists in this world apart from the influence of <term>action</term> <note>original: "karma"</note>. From the <term>Pure Consciousness</term> <note>original: "Chaitanya"</note> when it is veiled by <term>Illusion</term> <note>original: "Mâyâ" — the creative power that makes the absolute appear as the material world</note>, all things in existence have evolved.

->Just as various creatures arise in their proper time for enjoyment,<-
->just as through a defect a pearl-shell appears to be silver,<-
->so through the fault of one's own actions is the world projected upon the Absolute. 43.<-

43. Just as various creatures are born at the appropriate time to experience the consequences of their <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karma"</note>; and just as, due to a visual error, a piece of mother-of-pearl is mistaken for silver, so too, through the distorting influence of one's own past actions, a person mistakes the <term>Ultimate Reality</term> <note>original: "Brahman"</note> for the material universe.

<summary>This page explains how the individual soul is bound to the physical body and the cycle of rebirth through the law of karma. It describes the world as a projection or misconception superimposed upon the Absolute Reality (Brahman) due to the soul's past actions and the power of Illusion (Maya).</summary>

<keywords>Individual Soul, Jiva, Karma, Action, Merit, Sin, Illusion, Maya, Brahman, Absolute Reality, Superimposition, Rebirth</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 21 regarding the nature of the self and the causes of worldly suffering. It transitions from defining the soul's entrapment to explaining how spiritual knowledge acts as a remedy.</meta>

### SIVA SAMHITA

->The capacity for uprooting the delusions born of latent desires;<-
->When such knowledge arises, it is the means of attaining liberation. 44.<-

44. From **desire** <note>original: "vāsanā"</note> all these delusions arise; they can be eradicated with great difficulty; when the salvation-giving knowledge of the un-reality of the world arises, then are desires destroyed.

->When the direct Witness is seen, the delusion of the manifest disappears;<-
->There is no other way or cause; I tell you the truth, truly the truth. 45.<-

45. Being engrossed in the manifested (objective) world, the delusion arises about that which is the manifestor—the subject. There is no other cause of this delusion. Verily, verily, I tell you the truth.

->When the Manifestor is perceived, the illusion of the manifest is destroyed;<-
->For as long as one thinks "He exists not," the delusion of worldly existence never ceases. 46.<-

46. The illusion of the manifested (objective world) is destroyed when the **Maker of the Manifest** <note>The underlying consciousness or Divine that allows the world to be perceived.</note> becomes manifest. This illusion does not cease so long as one thinks, "**The Supreme Reality** <note>original: "Brahm"</note> is not."

->The cessation of false knowledge comes from direct observation of the truth;<-
->Otherwise it does not cease, just as the delusion of silver persists. 47.<-

47. By looking closely and deeply into the matter, this false knowledge vanishes. It cannot be removed otherwise; the **delusion of silver** <note>A classic philosophical metaphor: just as a person might mistake a shiny seashell (mother-of-pearl) for a silver coin in the sunlight, we mistake the physical world for the ultimate reality until we examine it closely.</note> remains.

->Until the knowledge of the stainless Manifestor arises;<-
->So long all beings and things appear separate and diverse. 48.<-

48. As long as knowledge does not arise about the stainless Manifestor of the universe, so long all things appear separate and many.

->When the body earned through past actions becomes a means for liberation;<-
->Then only does the carrying of the body become fruitful, and not otherwise. 49.<-

49. When this body, obtained through **action** <note>original: "karma"</note>, is made the means of obtaining **divine beatitude** <note>original: "Nirvāṇa"</note>; then only the carrying of the burden of the body becomes fruitful—not otherwise.

->Whatever original latent desire clings to and accompanies the individual soul;<-
->According to that, the creature suffers delusion in what should and should not be done. 50.<-

50. Of whatever nature is the original **latent desire** <note>original: "vāsanā"</note>, that clings to and accompanies the **individual soul** <note>original: "Jīva"</note> through various incarnations; similar is the delusion which it suffers, according to its deeds and misdeeds.

<summary>This page explains that the physical body and the delusions of the world are products of past actions and latent desires, which can only be overcome through direct spiritual knowledge of the "Manifestor." It emphasizes that the human body is only truly useful when used as a tool to achieve liberation (Nirvāṇa).</summary>

<keywords>liberation, latent desires, karma, individual soul, illusion, silver metaphor, Siva Samhita, Nirvāṇa, Jīva, vāsanā</keywords>

### CHAPTER II. 51-54.

->If a practitioner of **Yoga** <note>original: Yoga-sādhaka; a student or seeker practicing the discipline of spiritual union</note> wishes to cross the ocean of worldly existence,<-
->they should perform the duties of their <term>ashrama</term> <note>the four traditional stages of life: student, householder, forest-dweller, and renunciant</note> while renouncing the rewards of their actions. 51.<-

51. If the practitioner of **Yoga** wishes to cross the ocean of the world, he should perform all the duties of his <term>ashrama</term> (the condition of life), renouncing all the fruits of his works <note>This refers to the concept of Nishkama Karma, or acting without attachment to the results, as taught in the Bhagavad Gita</note>.

->Those attached to sensory objects and craving sensual pleasures<-
->are blocked from the path of **Nirvana** by the delusion of idle talk and fall into sinful deeds. 52.<-

52. Persons attached to sensual objects and desirous of sensual pleasures, descend from the road of <term>Nirvana</term> <note>original: Nirvâṇa; here referring to the state of final liberation and peace</note>, through the delusion of much talk, and fall into sinful deeds.

->Beholding the Self by the self, one sees nothing else here;<-
->then there is no fault in abandoning ritual works. This is my teaching. 53.<-

53. When a person does not see anything else here, having seen the Self by the self <note>This describes a state of non-duality where the individual soul recognizes its identity with the universal soul</note>; then there is no sin for him if he renounces all ritual works. This is my opinion.

->Desire and other impulses are dissolved through **Gnosis** only, and not otherwise;<-
->when all other principles cease to exist, the True Principle reveals itself. 54.<-

54. All desires and the rest are dissolved through <term>Gnosis</term> <note>original: Jñāna; spiritual wisdom or direct experiential knowledge of reality</note> only, and not otherwise. When all minor <term>tattvas</term> <note>fundamental principles or elements of reality</note> cease to exist, then My <term>Tattva</term> <note>the ultimate Reality or Essence</note> becomes manifest.

<summary>This page discusses the transition from performing social and religious duties to a state of pure spiritual knowledge, where ritual obligations are transcended through the realization of the Self.</summary>

# CHAPTER III.

## On Yoga Practice. The <term>Vāyus</term>.
<note>Vāyus (literally "winds") refers to the various forms of vital energy or life-force that circulate through the human body in Yogic physiology.</note>

->In the heart, there is a divine lotus adorned with a divine sign.<-
->It is endowed with the letters from *ka* to *tha*, decorated with these twelve syllables.<-
<note>original: "hṛdyasti paṅkajaṃ divyaṃ... kadiṭhāntākṣaropetaṃ"</note>

1. In the heart, there is a brilliant lotus with twelve petals adorned with brilliant signs. It has the letters from *k* to *th* <note>Specifically the Sanskrit consonants: k, kh, g, gh, n̄, ch, chh, j, jh, ñ, ṭ, ṭh.</note>, which are the twelve beautiful letters.

->The life-breath lives right there, adorned with various latent desires.<-
->Connected to beginningless karma and joined with the ego-sense.<-
<note>original: "prāṇo vasati tatraiva... prāpyāhaṅkārasaṃyutaḥ"</note>

2. The <term>Prāṇa</term> <note>The primary life-force or vital breath.</note> lives there, adorned with various desires, accompanied by the results of its past works that have no beginning, and joined with <term>Ahaṅkāra</term> <note>The "I-maker" or ego-sense that creates the feeling of individual identity.</note>.

<note>Note:—The heart is in the centre where there is the "seed" syllable *yam*.</note>

->Because of the different functions of the life-breath, it has various names.<-
->All of them exist there; it is not possible to mention them all.<-

3. From the different modifications and functions of the <term>Prāṇa</term>, it receives various names; all of them cannot be listed here.

->*Prāṇa, Apāna, Samāna, Udāna,* and the fifth is *Vyāna*.<-
->*Nāga, Kūrma, Kṛkara, Devadatta,* and *Dhanañjaya*.<-

4. These are the ten names: *Prāṇa, apāna, samāna, udāna, vyāna, nāga, kūrma, krikara, devadatta,* and *dhanañjaya.*

->These ten principal names are described by me in this scripture.<-
->They perform their functions here, incited by their own actions.<-

5. These are the ten principal names described by me in this <term>Śāstra</term> <note>A technical or religious treatise.</note>; they perform all the bodily functions, driven to do so by their own past actions and inherent natures.

->Among these ten, five breaths are the principal ones.<-
->And among those, I have declared *Prāṇa* and *Apāna* to be the highest agents.<-

6. Again, out of these ten, the first five are the leading ones; even among these, the *Prāṇa* and *Apāna* are the most important agents in my opinion.

->The *Prāṇa* is in the heart; the *Apāna* in the anus; the *Samāna* in the navel region.<-
->The *Udāna* is located in the throat; while the *Vyāna* moves through the whole body.<-

7. The seat of the *Prāṇa* is the heart; of the *apāna*, the lower region of the bowels; of the *samāna*, the region about the navel; of the *udāna*, the throat; while the *vyāna* moves throughout the entire body.

<summary>This page describes the mystical twelve-petaled lotus of the heart and introduces the ten Vāyus (vital airs), specifically locating the five primary energies within the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga practice, Prāṇa, Vāyus, heart chakra, vital airs, Ahaṅkāra, bodily functions, Yoga anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the bodily "winds" (vayu) from the previous section and begins a dedicated discourse on the spiritual importance of the Guru (teacher).</meta>

# CHAPTER III. 8-15.

->**The five minor vital airs, beginning with Naga, perform these functions in the body:**<-
->**Belching, opening the eyes, hunger and thirst, yawning, and fifthly, the hiccup. (8)**<-
<note>original: "nāgādavāyavaḥ" — The text refers to the five *upa-pranas* or secondary life forces: Naga (eructation), Kurma (opening eyes), Krikara (hunger/thirst), Devadatta (yawning), and Dhananjaya (hiccups/nourishment).</note>

8. The five remaining <term>vâyus</term> <note>vital airs or life forces</note>, the <term>nâga</term>, etc., perform the following functions in the body:—Eructation, opening the eyes, hunger and thirst, gaping or yawning, and lastly hiccup.

->**He who understands the body as a microcosm of the universe in this way,**<-
->**Is freed from all sins and reaches the highest spiritual state. (9)**<-
<note>The term *Brahmanda* literally means the "Egg of Brahma" (the Universe). The yogi is taught that the human body is a mirror of the entire cosmos.</note>

9. He who in this way knows the microcosm of the body, being absolved from all sins, reaches the highest state.

### (2).—*The Guru.*

->**Now I shall describe how to quickly achieve success in Yoga,**<-
->**Knowledge of which ensures that practitioners do not falter in their discipline. (10)**<-

10. Now I shall tell you, how easily to attain success in <term>Yoga</term>, by knowing which the <term>Yogis</term> <note>practitioners of yoga</note> never fail in the practice of Yoga.

->**Knowledge becomes powerful and effective only when received directly from the lips of a Teacher;**<-
->**Otherwise, it remains fruitless, weak, and may even lead to great suffering. (11)**<-
<note>original: "Guru-vaktra-samudbhava" — literally "born from the mouth of the Guru." This emphasizes the necessity of the oral tradition and personal initiation over self-study.</note>

11. Only the knowledge imparted by a <term>Guru</term> <note>spiritual teacher</note>, through his lips, is powerful and useful; otherwise it becomes fruitless, weak and very painful.

->**Those who pursue knowledge with effort while pleasing their Teacher,**<-
->**Will surely obtain the fruits of that knowledge through his support. (12)**<-

12. He who is devoted to any knowledge, while pleasing his Guru with every attention, readily obtains the fruit of that knowledge.

->**The Teacher is the father, the Teacher is the mother, and the Teacher is undoubtedly God Himself;**<-
->**Therefore, he should be served by everyone in thought, word, and deed. (13)**<-

13. There is not the least doubt that Guru is father, Guru is mother, and Guru is God even; and as such, he should be served by all with their thought, word and deed.

->**Through the grace of the Teacher, one obtains everything that is good for the self.**<-
->**Therefore, the Teacher must be served daily, for without him, nothing auspicious can happen. (14)**<-

14. By Guru's favour everything good relating to one's self is obtained. So the Guru ought to be daily served; else there can be nothing auspicious.

->**Having circled the Teacher three times and touched his lotus-feet with the right hand,**<-
->**One should perform a prostration using all eight limbs of the body. (15)**<-
<note>original: "pradakṣiṇatrayaṃ" — the act of circumambulation, keeping the object of reverence on one's right side.</note>

15. Let him salute his Guru after walking three times round him, and touching with his right hand his <term>lotus-feet</term> <note>a poetic term for the feet of a deity or teacher, symbolizing purity</note>.

<summary>This page lists the physical functions of the five minor vital airs and establishes the Guru as the essential source of spiritual success, comparing the teacher to parents and divinity.</summary>
<keywords>vital airs, vayu, naga, microcosm, Guru, devotion, yoga practice, ashtanga prostration, spiritual teacher</keywords>

<meta>This page outlines the qualifications required for a student to succeed in Yoga and describes the ideal environment for practice, continuing the instructional tone of the Siva Samhita.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA.

### (3).--The Qualified Student. <note>original: "Adhikâri" — a term referring to a person who has attained the necessary level of readiness or eligibility for spiritual study.</note>

->**Success is certain for those who possess faith and self-control;**<-
->**others will not succeed. Therefore, one should practice Yoga with diligence. 16.**<-

16. A person who exercises self-control will surely achieve success through faith; no one else can succeed. Therefore, with faith, <term>Yoga</term> <note>Yoga: From the root 'yuj,' meaning to join or yoke; a disciplined practice for uniting the individual soul with the universal consciousness.</note> should be practiced with care and steady perseverance.

->**Success will not come to those attached to worldly company, nor to the skeptical;**<-
->**nor to those who lack devotion to their teacher, nor to those who frequent large crowds.**<-
->**Nor to those who delight in lies, or those who speak cruelly;**<-
->**those who fail to please their teacher will never attain success. 17.**<-

17. Those who are addicted to sensual pleasures or keep bad company, who are cynical or disbelieving, who lack respect for their <term>Guru</term> <note>Guru: A spiritual guide or teacher; literally "the one who removes darkness."</note>, who frequent rowdy or promiscuous gatherings, who are addicted to false and vain arguments, who are harsh in their speech, or who do not satisfy the requirements of their teacher, will never reach the goal.

->**The first sign of success is the firm belief that it will bear fruit;**<-
->**the second is faith; the third is devotion to the teacher;**<-
->**the fourth is a spirit of universal equality; the fifth is control of the senses;**<-
->**the sixth is moderate eating. There is no seventh condition. 18.**<-

18. The first condition of success is the firm belief that this <term>vidyâ</term> <note>vidyâ: Meaning "knowledge," "science," or "wisdom," specifically referring here to the sacred science of Yoga.</note> must be successful and fruitful; the second condition is having deep faith in the practice; the third is respect for the teacher; the fourth is maintaining a spirit of universal equality toward all; the fifth is the restraint of the sense organs; the sixth is <term>mitâhâra</term> <note>mitâhâra: The practice of moderate or measured eating, essential for maintaining the body's balance during Yoga.</note>. These are the only requirements; there is no seventh.

->**Having received instructions in Yoga and finding a teacher who knows Yoga,**<-
->**one should practice with a focused mind, following the method the teacher taught. 19.**<-

19. Once a student has received instructions in <term>Yoga</term> and found a teacher who is themselves a master of the art, they should practice with earnestness and faith, following the specific methods provided by that teacher.

### (4).--The Place and Preparation.

->**In a beautiful, secluded room, the practitioner, in the Lotus Pose,**<-
->**sitting upon a seat, should practice the regulation of the breath. 20.**<-

20. Let the <term>Yogi</term> <note>Yogi: One who practices Yoga.</note> go to a beautiful and pleasant place of retirement or a quiet cell. There, they should assume the <term>padmâsana</term> <note>padmâsana: The "Lotus Pose," a cross-legged sitting position intended to keep the spine straight and the body stable.</note> posture and, sitting on a seat <note>Often specified in tradition as being made of kusa grass or a deer skin to insulate the practitioner's energy.</note>, begin the practice of breath regulation.

<summary>This page defines the moral and mental prerequisites for a Yoga student, emphasizing faith, discipline, and devotion to a teacher, while introducing the basic physical requirements for beginning breath-work.</summary>
<keywords>Adhikari, Yoga requirements, Guru-disciple relationship, Vidya, Mitahara, Padmasana, breath regulation, spiritual discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the practitioner (Sadhaka) beginning their formal yoga practice, following the preliminary postures and mental preparations described in previous verses.</meta>

# CHAPTER III. 21-26.

---

->With the body held straight and hands joined in a gesture of reverence, the wise student bows to his teachers.<-
->On his right, he honors <term>Ganesha</term> <note>The elephant-headed deity traditionally invoked at the start of any endeavor to remove obstacles.</note>; on his left, he honors the Guardians of the World and the Goddess <term>Ambika</term> <note>A name for the Divine Mother or Shakti.</note>. (21)<-

21. The wise beginner should keep his body firm and steady, his hands joined as if in supplication, and salute the <term>Gurus</term> <note>Spiritual teachers or guides.</note> on the left side. He should also pay salutations to <term>Ganesha</term> on the right side, and again to the guardians of the worlds and the goddess <term>Ambika</term>, who are on the left side.

### (5).—The <term>Pranayama</term> <note>The practice of breath control; literally "extension of the life-force."</note>.

->Then, closing the <term>Pingala</term> with the right thumb, the wise one<-
->should fill the body with air through the <term>Ida</term> and retain the breath as long as possible.<-
->Then, he should release the breath through the <term>Pingala</term> slowly, not with force. (22)<-

22. Then let the wise practitioner close with his right thumb the <term>Pingala</term> <note>original: पिङ्गला (piṅgalā); the "solar" energy channel corresponding to the right nostril.</note>, inspire air through the <term>Ida</term> <note>original: इडा (iḍā); the "lunar" energy channel corresponding to the left nostril.</note>; and keep the air confined—suspend his breathing—as long as he can; and afterwards let him breathe out slowly, and not forcibly, through the right nostril.

->Again, filling through the <term>Pingala</term>, he should retain the breath as long as possible.<-
->He should then expel the air through the <term>Ida</term>, not forcibly, but very slowly. (23)<-

23. Again, let him draw breath through the right nostril, and stop breathing as long as his strength permits; then let him expel the air through the left nostril, not forcibly, but slowly and gently.

->According to the rules of Yoga, he should perform twenty <term>Kumbhakas</term>.<-
->Free from all dualities, he should practice daily without laziness. (24)<-

24. According to the above method of <term>Yoga</term>, let him practise twenty <term>kumbhakas</term> <note>original: कुम्भक (kumbhaka); the phase of breath retention.</note>. He should practise this daily without neglect or idleness, and free from all duals <note>Referring to 'dvandvas' or pairs of opposites such as heat/cold, pleasure/pain, or attraction/repulsion.</note>, etc.

->In the morning, at midday, at sunset, and at midnight:<-
->He should perform these <term>Kumbhakas</term> four times a day at these specific hours. (25)<-

25. These <term>kumbhakas</term> should be practised four times:—once (1) early in the morning at sun-rise, (2) then at mid-day, (3) the third at sun-set, and (4) the fourth at mid-night.

->In this way, if practiced daily for three months without laziness,<-
->the purification of the <term>Nadis</term> will certainly occur without delay. (26)<-

26. When this has been practised daily, for three months, with regularity, the <term>nadis</term> <note>original: नाडी (nāḍī); the subtle channels or vessels through which vital energy flows.</note> of the body will readily and surely be purified.

<summary>This page details the specific technique of alternate nostril breathing (Pranayama) and breath retention (Kumbhaka), prescribing a rigorous schedule of four sessions per day.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, breath retention, Kumbhaka, Ida, Pingala, Nadi purification, Yoga practice, Ganesha, Ambika</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions on the purification of the subtle body and the onset of the first stage of yogic practice.</meta>

# THE COMPENDIUM OF SHIVA (SIVA SAMHITA)

->When the <term>nadis</term> <note>subtle energy channels through which life force flows</note> of the truth-perceiving <term>Yogi</term> <note>a practitioner of yoga</note> are purified, then, with all defects destroyed, the commencement stage begins.<- 
<note>original: "यदा तु नाडीशुद्धिः स्याद्योगिनस्तत्त्वदर्शिनः । तदा विध्वस्तदोषश्च भवेदारम्भसम्भवः ॥ २७ ॥"</note>

27. When thus the <term>nadis</term> of the truth-perceiving <term>Yogi</term> are purified, then his defects being all destroyed, he enters the first stage in the practice of <term>Yoga</term> called <term>arambha</term> <note>literally "the beginning" or commencement</note>.

->Signs appear in the body of the <term>Yogi</term> whose <term>nadis</term> have been purified. I shall now describe all these various signs in brief.<- 
<note>original: "चिह्नानि योगिनो देहे दृश्यन्ते नाडिशुद्धितः । कथ्यन्ते तु समस्तान्यङ्गानि संक्षेपतो मया ॥ २८ ॥"</note>

28. Certain signs are perceived in the body of the <term>Yogi</term> whose <term>nadis</term> have been purified. I shall describe, in brief, all these various signs.

->A balanced body, a sweet scent, a beautiful luster, and a melodic voice; the Commencement, the Vessel, and likewise Familiarity; and Perfection in all yogas—these are the stages of <term>Yoga</term>.<- 
<note>original: "समकायः सुगन्धिश्च सुकान्तिः स्वरसाधकः । आरम्भघटकश्चैव यथा परिचयस्तदा ॥ निष्पत्तिः सर्वयोगेषु योगावस्था भवन्ति ताः ॥ २९ ॥"</note>

29. The body of the person practicing the regulation of breath becomes harmoniously developed, emits a sweet scent, and looks beautiful and lovely. In all kinds of <term>Yoga</term>, there are four stages of <term>pranayama</term> <note>the practice of breath control</note>:— 1, <term>Arambha-avastha</term> (the state of beginning); 2, <term>Ghata-avastha</term> <note>the "vessel" stage, where the body is prepared to hold energy</note> (the state of co-operation of Self and Higher Self); 3, <term>Parichaya-avastha</term> <note>the "familiarity" or intimacy stage</note> (knowledge); 4, <term>Nishpatti-avastha</term> (the final consummation or perfection).

->We have already spoken of the Commencement for the mastery of the breath; the others, which destroy the flood of all sorrows, will be told later.<- 
<note>original: "आरम्भः कथितोऽस्माभिरधुना वायुसिद्धये । अपरः कथ्यते पश्चात्सर्वदुःखौघनाशनः ॥ ३० ॥"</note>

30. We have already described the beginning or <term>Arambha-avastha</term> of <term>pranayama</term>; the rest will be described hereafter. They destroy all sin and sorrow.

->A strong digestive fire, enjoyment, happiness, beauty in every limb, a full heart, and the presence of all enthusiasm and strength—these surely arise in the body of the <term>Yogi</term>.<- 
<note>original: "प्रौढवह्निः सुभोगी च सुखीसर्वाङ्गसुन्दरः । सम्पूर्णहृदयो योगी सर्वोत्साहबलान्वितः ॥ जायते योगिनोऽवश्यमेतत्सर्वं कलेवरे ॥ ३१ ॥"</note>

31. The following qualities are surely always found in the bodies of every <term>Yogi</term>:—Strong appetite, good digestion, cheerfulness, a handsome figure, great courage, mighty enthusiasm, and full strength.

->Now I shall speak of what must be avoided, the great obstacles to <term>Yoga</term>, by crossing which the <term>Yogis</term> pass over the ocean of worldly suffering.<- 
<note>original: "अथ वर्ज्यं प्रवक्ष्यामि योगविघ्नकरं परम् । येन संसारदुःखान्धिं तीर्त्वा यास्यन्ति योगिन ॥ ३२ ॥"</note>

32. Now I tell you the great obstacles to <term>Yoga</term> which must be avoided, as by their removal the <term>Yogis</term> cross easily this sea of worldly sorrow.

->(6).—*The things to be renounced.*<-

->Sour, dry, sharp, salty, mustard, and bitter foods; excessive walking, bathing at dawn, and oils that cause burning.<- 
<note>original: "अम्लं रूक्षं तथा तीक्ष्णं लवणं सार्षपं कटुम् । बहुलं भ्रमणं प्रातः स्नानं तैलविदाहकम् ॥"</note>

<summary>This page details the physical and spiritual indicators of successful energy channel purification and introduces the four progressive stages of Yoga. It also begins listing the dietary and lifestyle prohibitions necessary for a practitioner to overcome worldly suffering.</summary>
<keywords>purification of nadis, yoga stages, arambha-avastha, ghata-avastha, parichaya-avastha, nishpatti-avastha, yogic signs, dietary restrictions, pranayama</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the practitioner of Yoga, moving from prohibitions to the positive habits and conditions required for success. It follows the previous discussion on the preliminary stages of practice.</meta>

# CHAPTER III. 33-36.

---

> <gloss>Theft, violence, hatred of people, pride, dishonesty, fasting, untruth, [distractions from] liberation, and harming living beings; companionship with women, worship of fire, much talking, whether pleasant or unpleasant; and overeating—the Yogi must certainly renounce these things. || 33 ||</gloss>

33. The Yogi should renounce the following:—1. Acids, 2. astringents, 3. pungent substances, 4. salt, 5. mustard, and 6. bitter things; 7. much walking, 8. early bathing (before sunrise) and 9. things roasted in oil; 10. theft, 11. killing (of animals), 12. enmity towards any person, 13. pride, 14. duplicity, and 15. crookedness; 16. fasting, 17. untruth, 18. thoughts other than those of <term>moksha</term> <note>original: "moksha"; refers to spiritual liberation or enlightenment. In this context, the practitioner is told to avoid any mental state that does not lead toward this goal</note>, 19. cruelty towards animals; 20. companionship of women, 21. worship of (or handling or sitting near) fire <note>ritual fire sacrifices were common, but the intense heat was thought to disturb the body's internal balance for Yoga</note>, and 22. much talking, without regard to the pleasantness or unpleasantness of speech, and lastly, 23. much eating.

### (7). — The means.

> <gloss>I shall now speak of the means for the swift success of Yoga; it must be kept secret by practitioners so that success is truly attained. || 34 ||</gloss>

34. Now I will tell you the means by which success in <term>Yoga</term> <note>the discipline of uniting the individual soul with the universal</note> is quickly obtained; it must be kept secret by the practitioner so that success may come with certainty.

> <gloss>Clarified butter, milk, sweet food, betel without lime, camphor, kind words, a pleasant monastery with small openings; hearing the established truths, practicing non-attachment while in the home, chanting the names of Vishnu, and hearing the supreme inner sounds; patience, forgiveness, austerity, purity, modesty, devotion, and service to the teacher—the great Yogi should always practice these observances. || 35 ||</gloss>

35. The great Yogi should observe always the following observances:—He should use 1. clarified butter <note>ghee</note>, 2. milk, 3. sweet food, and 4. betel without lime <note>the "lime" used in traditional betel chewing is calcium hydroxide, which the text advises against here</note>, 5. camphor; 6. kind words, 7. pleasant monastery or retired cell, having a small door; 8. hear discourses on truth, and 9. always discharge his household duties with <term>vairâgya</term> <note>non-attachment or dispassion toward worldly outcomes</note>, 10. sing the name of Vishnu <note>the Preserver deity in the Hindu trinity</note>; 11. and hear sweet music <note>this refers to "sunada," the internal sacred sounds heard during meditation</note>, 12. have patience, 13. constancy, 14. forgiveness, 15. austerities, 16. purifications, 17. modesty, 18. devotion, and 19. service of the <term>Guru</term> <note>the spiritual teacher</note>.

> <gloss>When the air enters the "sun," the Yogi should take his food; when the air has entered the "moon," the best of practitioners should sleep. || 36 ||</gloss>

36. When the air enters the sun, it is the proper time for the Yogi to take his food (*i.e.*, when the breath flows through the <term>Pingalâ</term> <note>the 'solar' energy channel, corresponding to the right nostril, which is associated with digestion and heat</note>); when the air enters the moon <note>the 'lunar' channel or <term>Ida</term>, corresponding to the left nostril, associated with cooling and rest</note>, the practitioner should sleep.

<summary>This page outlines the specific dietary and lifestyle prohibitions for a Yogi, alongside a list of positive habits ("The Means") and environmental conditions necessary for spiritual success. It also introduces the timing of eating and sleeping based on the flow of breath through the solar and lunar nostrils.</summary>

<keywords>renunciation, Yoga diet, Moksha, Vairagya, Vishnu, Guru, Pingala, Ida, Nadi, lifestyle restrictions</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for breath regulation (prāṇāyāma) found in the Shiva Samhita, specifically focusing on the physical signs of progress and dietary requirements for the practitioner.</meta>

-># SHIVA SAMHITA<-

When the air enters the "moon" (the left nostril), the practitioner should go to rest—that is, when the breath flows through the <term>Iḍâ: the energetic channel on the left side of the body associated with cooling, lunar energy</term>.

The wise do not practice when they have just eaten, nor when they are very hungry. At the beginning of practice, one should first consume a meal of milk and clarified butter (ghee). <note>Original Sanskrit: *sadyo bhukte'pi kṣudhite nābhyāsaḥ kriyate budhaiḥ | abhyāsakāle prathamaṃ kuryātkṣīrājyabhojanam*</note>

37. Yoga practice <term>prânâyâma: the science of breath control</term> should not be performed immediately after meals, nor when one is *very hungry*; before starting the practice, some milk and butter should be taken.

Once the practice becomes steady and firm, such strict rules no longer need to be followed. The practitioner should eat small amounts many times a day, and should perform breath retention every day at the previously mentioned times. <note>Original Sanskrit: *tato'bhyāse sthirībhūte na tādṛṅniyamagrahaḥ | abhyāsinā vibhoktavyaṃ stokaṃ stokamanekadhā*</note>

38. When one is well established in his practice, then he need not observe these restrictions. The practitioner should eat in small quantities at a time, though frequently; and should practice <term>kumbhaka: the intentional retention of breath</term> daily at the stated times.

Then the Yogi gains the power to control the air as they wish. Through this mastery of the breath, success in retention is certainly achieved. Once the highest form of breath retention is mastered, what is there in this world that the Yogi cannot achieve? <note>Original Sanskrit: *tato yatheṣṭā śaktiḥ syādyogino vāyudhāraṇe...*</note>

39. When the Yogi can, of his will, regulate the air and stop the breath (whenever and for however long) he likes, then certainly he gets success in *kumbhaka*, and from the success in *kumbhaka* only, what things cannot the Yogi command here?

### The first stage.

In the first efforts of the Yogi, perspiration is produced in the body. When this sweat appears, the wise practitioner should rub it into the limbs. Otherwise, the vital essences of the Yogi's body are lost. <note>Original Sanskrit: *svedaḥ saṃjāyate dehe yoginaḥ prathamodyame...*</note>

40. In the first stage of breath control, the body of the Yogi begins to perspire. When it perspires, he should rub it well into the skin; otherwise, the body of the Yogi loses its <term>dhâtu: the fundamental humors or vital restorative fluids of the body</term>.

### The second and third stages.

In the second stage, there is a trembling of the body. In the middle (third) stage, the jumping of a frog is observed. From even more intense practice, the seeker eventually moves through the sky. <note>Original Sanskrit: *dvitīye hi bhavetkampo dārdurī madhyame matā...*</note>

41. In the second stage, the body begins to tremble; in the third, there is "frog-jumping" <note>referred to in the text as *dârdurî*</note>; and when the practice becomes even more advanced, the student is able to walk in the air.

### Mastery of the Air (Vâyusiddhi).

The Yogi, even while remaining in the Lotus Posture, can leave the ground and remain suspended. This mastery of the air should be known as the destroyer of the darkness of this world. <note>Original Sanskrit: *yogī padmāsanastho'pi bhuvamutsṛjya vartate...*</note>

42. Even while sitting in the <term>Padmasana: the cross-legged "Lotus" seated posture</term>, the Yogi rises up from the earth and remains there. This state is <term>Vâyusiddhi: the power of mastery over the air</term>, which destroys the ignorance of worldly existence.

<summary>This page outlines the progression of breath control (prāṇāyāma), describing physical milestones from sweating and trembling to "frog-jumping" and eventually levitation (Vâyusiddhi). It also provides dietary advice, recommending milk and ghee for beginners while emphasizing the importance of frequent, small meals.</summary>
<keywords>prāṇāyāma, kumbhaka, Iḍâ, dhâtu, Vâyusiddhi, levitation, Yoga diet, Shiva Samhita, breath retention, Lotus posture</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions of Chapter III, focusing on the physical signs of progress in pranayama and the transition to higher meditative states.</meta>

### CHAPTER III. 42-48.

42. When the Yogi, though remaining in the <term>Lotus Posture<note>original: Padmâsana</note></term>, can rise in the air and leave the ground, then know that he has gained <term>success over the air<note>original: Vâyu-siddhi</note></term>, which destroys the darkness of the world.

->**During this time, one should strictly observe the rules and restrictions of Yoga.**<-
->**Sleep becomes light, and bodily waste and urine are diminished.**<-

43. But so long as he does not gain this power, let him continue practicing and observing all the rules and restrictions laid down above. From the perfection of <term>breath control<note>original: prânâyâma</note></term>, there follows a decrease in sleep, solid waste, and urine.

->**The Yogi who perceives the Truth becomes free from disease and sorrow.**<-
->**Sweat, saliva, and parasites no longer arise within him.**<-

44. The truth-perceiving Yogi becomes free from disease, and sorrow or affliction; he never suffers from foul perspiration, excess saliva, or intestinal worms.

->**When the humors of phlegm, bile, and wind are balanced in the practitioner's body,**<-
->**At that stage, the seeker is no longer bound by dietary restrictions.**<-

45. When in the body of the practitioner, there is no longer an imbalance of phlegm, wind, or bile <note>These are the three "doshas" or bodily humors in traditional Indian medicine; balance among them indicates perfect health.</note>; then he may with impunity be irregular in his diet and other habits.

->**Whether eating very little or a great deal, the Yogi does not suffer.**<-
->**Through the power of practice, the Yogi attains "earth-walking" success.**<-
->**He moves just as a frog jumps across the ground when startled by a clap.**<-

46. No harmful results would follow, even if the Yogi were to take a large quantity of food, or very little, or no food at all. Through the strength of constant practice, the Yogi obtains <term>earth-walking success<note>original: Bhuchari-siddhi</note></term>; he moves by hopping across the ground, much like a frog jumps when frightened by the clapping of hands.

->**In this path, there are many terrible and unavoidable obstacles.**<-
->**Nevertheless, the Yogi should persevere even if his life-breath reaches his throat.**<-

47. Verily, there are many difficult and almost insurmountable obstacles in the path of Yoga, yet the Yogi should go on with his practice at all hazards; even if he feels he is at the very point of death.

->**Therefore, sitting in a secluded place with senses restrained,**<-
->**The practitioner should chant the sacred syllable at length to destroy all obstacles.**<-

48. Then let the practitioner, sitting in a retired place and restraining his senses, utter by inaudible repetition the long <term>sacred syllable<note>original: pranava</note></term> **OM**, in order to destroy all obstacles.

<note>The three components of the sound—A, U, and M—should each be uttered distinctly.</note>

<summary>This page details the physical milestones of advanced breath control, including levitation, the balancing of bodily humors, and the ability to jump like a frog, while emphasizing the importance of chanting OM to overcome spiritual obstacles.</summary>
<keywords>levitation, Lotus Posture, breath control, physical purification, diet, earth-walking success, OM, obstacles in Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Prāṇāyāma (breath regulation) and its spiritual and physical benefits as described in the Siva Samhita, a classical Sanskrit text on Hatha Yoga.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA.

->**The wise practitioner surely destroys all actions and their consequences, whether acquired in this life or in the past, through the regulation of breath.**<-
->**49**<-

49. The wise practitioner surely destroys all his <term>karma</term> <note>the law of cause and effect where intent and actions influence the future</note>, whether acquired in this life or in the past, through the regulation of breath <note>original: prāṇāyāma</note>.

->**The great practitioner of Yoga destroys the various virtues and vices accumulated in his past life by sixteen breath-regulations.**<-
->**50**<-

50. The great <term>Yogi</term> <note>an advanced practitioner of yoga</note> destroys by sixteen <term>prânâyâmas</term> <note>cycles of breath control</note> the various virtues and vices accumulated in his past life.

->**He burns away the heap of cotton-like sins with the fire of the great dissolution; then, freed from sin, he afterwards destroys his merits.**<-
->**51**<-

51. This <term>prânâyâma</term> destroys sin, as fire burns away a heap of cotton; it makes the <term>Yogi</term> free from sin; next it destroys the bonds of all his good actions <note>In this tradition, both good and bad deeds create attachments that bind a soul to the cycle of rebirth; both must be transcended for liberation</note>.

->**The mighty master of Yoga, having attained the eight-fold psychic powers through breath-regulation and having crossed the ocean of virtue and vice, moves about freely through the three worlds.**<-
->**52**<-

52. The mighty <term>Yogi</term> having attained, through <term>prânâyâma</term>, the eight sorts of psychic powers <note>original: aiśvaryāṣṭakāni, referring to the classical 'Siddhis' like becoming infinitely small or large</note>, and having crossed the ocean of virtue and vice, moves about freely through the three worlds <note>traditionally: heaven, earth, and the netherworld</note>.

### *Increase of duration.*

->**Then, through the sequence of practice, the duration should reach three ghatikas; by this, all the perfections desired by the practitioner will certainly occur.**<-
->**53**<-

53. Then gradually he should make himself able to practise for three <term>gharis</term> <note>a ghari is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes; three gharis equal 72 minutes or roughly an hour and a half</note> (one hour and a half at a time, he should be able to restrain breath for that period). Through this, the <term>Yogi</term> undoubtedly obtains all the longed-for powers.

### *Siddhis or Perfections.*

->**The power of speech, moving at will, far-seeing, and likewise far-hearing, subtle vision, and entering another’s body; turning things to gold by smearing them with waste, as well as invisibility; all these occur, and the ability to move through the sky for practitioners.**<-
->**54**<-

54. The <term>Yogi</term> acquires the following <term>Siddhis</term> <note>supernatural powers or perfections</note>: <term>prophecy</term> <note>original: vâkya siddhi; the power to make whatever one says come true</note>, transporting himself everywhere at will <note>original: Kâmachâri</note>, clairvoyance <note>original: duradristhi; literally 'far-seeing'</note>, clairaudience <note>original: durashruti; literally 'far-hearing'</note>, subtle-sight <note>original: shukshma-drishti</note>, and the power of entering another's body <note>original: parakâyapravesana</note>, turning base metals to gold by rubbing them with his excrements and urine <note>This likely refers to alchemical metaphors common in medieval Hatha Yoga texts</note>, and the power of becoming invisible, and lastly, moving in the air <note>original: khecharatva</note>.

<summary>This page describes how the practice of breath regulation (Pranayama) purifies the practitioner of past karma and grants supernatural abilities (Siddhis), such as clairvoyance and the power to move through the air.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Karma, Yogi, Siddhis, psychic powers, clairvoyance, breath control, spiritual liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four stages of Hatha Yoga practice (Avasthas), following the initial "Beginning" (Arumbha) stage. It details the second and third stages: the "Jar" (Ghata) and "Familiarity" (Parichaya) states.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
### CHAPTER III. 55-60.

---

### II — The "Jar" State (Ghata Avastha).

**When the highest state of <term>Ghata</term> (the "Jar") is achieved through the practice of <term>Pranayama</term> (regulated breathing), then in this circle of worldly existence, there is nothing that the practitioner cannot accomplish.**
<note>original: "यदा भवेद्धटावस्था पवनाभ्यासने परा । तदा संसारचक्रेऽस्मिन्नास्ति यन्न साधयेत् ॥ ५५ ॥"</note>

**55.** When, by the practice of <term>Pranayama</term> (the science of breath control), the Yogi reaches the state of <term>ghata</term> (resembling a water-jar in stillness), then for him there is nothing in this cycle of the universe which he cannot achieve.

**The "Jar" is so called because it is the state where the <term>Prana</term> (vital breath), the <term>Apana</term> (downward energy), the <term>Nada</term> (inner sound), the <term>Vindu</term> (the creative point), the <term>Jivatma</term> (the Individual Soul), and the <term>Paramatma</term> (the Supreme Soul) meet and unite.**
<note>original: "प्राणापाननादबिंदुजीवात्मपरमात्मनः । मिलित्वा घटते यस्मात्तस्माद्वै घट उच्यते ॥ ५६ ॥"</note>

**56.** The *ghata* is defined as that state in which the <term>prana</term> and the <term>apana</term> (the two primary internal winds), the <term>nada</term> (mystical inner sound) and the <term>vindu</term> (the concentrated seed of consciousness), and the <term>jivatma</term> (the human spirit) and the <term>paramatma</term> (the universal spirit) combine and work in harmony.

**When one is capable of holding the breath for three hours, then the wonderful state of <term>Pratyahara</term> is certainly attained; it cannot be achieved otherwise.**
<note>original: "याममात्रं यदा धर्तुं समर्थः स्यात्तदाद्भुतः । प्रत्याहारस्तदैव स्यान्नांतरा भवति ध्रुवम् ॥ ५७ ॥"</note>

**57.** When the practitioner gains the power of holding the breath—that is, entering a state of deep meditative trance—for three hours, then the remarkable state of <term>pratyahara</term> (the withdrawal of the senses from external objects) is reached without fail.

**Whatever object the King of Yogis perceives, let him contemplate it as the Self. When the functions of the various senses are mastered, then victory over the senses is won.**
<note>original: "यं यं जानाति योगीन्द्रस्तं तमात्मेति भावयेत् । यैरिन्द्रियैर्विधानास्तदिन्द्रियजयो भवेत् ॥ ५८ ॥"</note>

**58.** Whatever object the master Yogi perceives, he should view it as an extension of his own spirit. Once the mechanics and actions of the different senses are understood, they can be fully conquered.

**When, through intense practice, a single <term>Kumbhaka</term> can be held for a full three hours—when the Yogi’s breath remains steady for eight <term>Dandas</term>—then the wise one can balance himself on his thumb by his own power, though he may appear to others as if he were insane.**
<note>original: "याममात्रं यदा पूर्णं भवेद्भ्यासयोगतः । एकवारं प्रकुर्वीत तदा योगी च कुम्भकम् ॥ दण्डाष्टकं यदा वायुनिश्चलो योगिनो भवेत् । स्वसामर्थ्यात्तदांगुष्ठे तिष्ठेद्वातुलवत्सुधीः ॥ ५९ ॥"</note>

**59.** When, through diligent practice, the Yogi can perform a single <term>kumbhaka</term> (breath retention) for a full three hours, and when for eight <term>dandas</term> (a traditional unit of time totaling 3 hours) the breath is suspended and still, then that wise practitioner can balance his entire body on his thumb; however, to the uninitiated observer, he appears to be out of his mind.

### III.—The "Familiarity" State (Parichaya).

**Next, through continued practice, the Yogi reaches the state of <term>Parichaya</term> (Familiarity). When the breath, leaving the "Sun" and "Moon," remains steady in the void of the <term>Sushumna</term>, then the breath is said to be "known."**
<note>original: "ततः परिचयावस्था योगिनोऽभ्यासतो भवेत् । यदा वायुश्चन्द्रसूर्यौ त्यक्त्वा तिष्ठति निश्चलम् ॥ वायुः परिचितो वायुः सुषुम्ना व्योम्नि संचरेत् ॥ ६० ॥"</note>

**60.** After this, through consistent exercise, the Yogi enters the <term>parichaya avastha</term>. When the breath leaves the <term>Pingala</term> (the "Sun" or right nostril) and <term>Ida</term> (the "Moon" or left nostril) and remains motionless within the etheric space of the <term>sushumna</term> (the central psychic channel), the practitioner has reached the stage of familiarity with the life force.

<summary>This page describes the advanced stages of yoga practice where the practitioner achieves total internal union (the Jar state) and masters the ability to suspend breath for long periods, leading to the mastery of the central psychic channel.</summary>

<keywords>Ghata Avastha, breath retention, sense withdrawal, Parichaya stage, Sushumna, spiritual union, Kumbhaka, Pranayama</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 33, this section describes the advanced stages of Yoga practice, specifically the 'Parichaya' (familiarity) state and the mastery over physical elements.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA.

-> Having definitely grasped the power of action and pierced through the energy centers, when the state of familiarity arises through the yoga of practice, || <-
-> Then the Yogi certainly perceives the three-fold heap of his past actions. || 61 || <-

61. When he, by the practice of <term>Yoga</term> <note>a system of spiritual and physical discipline</note>, acquires the power of action (<italic><term>kriyâ shakti</term></italic> <note>original Sanskrit: "kriyâ shakti"; the energy of manifestation or movement</note>) and pierces through the six <term>chakras</term> <note>energy centers located along the spine in the subtle body</note>, and reaches the sure condition of <term>parichaya</term> <note>the third stage of yoga, meaning "familiarity" or "intimacy" with the practice</note>, then the <term>Yogi</term>, verily, sees the three-fold effects of <term>karma</term> <note>the spiritual law of cause and effect resulting from past deeds</note>.

---

-> And then, let him destroy the heaps of karma with the sacred syllable OM. <-
-> That Yogi should perform the "body-multiplication" to experience the fruits of his actions. || 62 || <-

62. Then, let the <term>Yogi</term> destroy the multitude of <term>karmas</term> by the <term>pranava</term> <note>the sacred syllable <term>OM</term></note>; let him accomplish <italic><term>kâyavyuha</term></italic> <note>original Sanskrit: "kâyavyuha"; a mystical yogic process of creating multiple bodies or arranging the body’s components to exhaust karma</note> (a mystical process of arranging the various <term>skandas</term> <note>the five aggregates or components that constitute a person</note> of the body), in order to enjoy or suffer the consequences of all his actions in one life, without the necessity of re-birth.

---

-> At this time, the great Yogi should practice the five-fold concentration. <-
-> By which the mastery over earth and the other elements is attained, removing the fear of the elements. || 63 || <-

63. At that time let the great <term>Yogi</term> practice the five-fold <term>dhâranâ</term> <note>intense concentration or "holding" of the mind</note> forms of concentration on <term>Vishnu</term> <note>the preservation aspect of the Divine</note>, by which command over the five elements is obtained, and fear of injuries from any one of them is removed. (Earth, water, fire, air, and <italic><term>Akas</term></italic> <note>ether or space</note> cannot harm him.)

<note>Note.—He should perform 5 <term>Kumbhakas</term> <note>breath-retentions</note> at each centre or <term>Chakra</term>.</note>

---

-> Five periods in the base, and likewise in the seat of the linga. <-
-> Above that, five periods in the navel and the heart centers likewise. <-
-> In the throat and above in the middle of the brows, the wise one should hold for five periods. <-
-> Thus the King of Yogis will surely not be destroyed by the earth and other elements. || 64 || <-

64. Let the wise <term>Yogi</term> practice <italic><term>dhâranâ</term></italic> <note>concentration</note> thus:—five <term>ghatis</term> <note>a unit of time; one ghati is approximately 24 minutes, so five ghatis equals 2 hours</note> in the <italic>base lotus</italic> (<term>Mulâdhara</term> <note>the root energy center at the base of the spine</note>); five <term>ghatis</term> in the seat of the <term>linga</term> (<term>Svâdhisthânâ</term> <note>the sacral energy center</note>), five <term>ghatis</term> in the region above it, (in the navel, <term>Manipur</term> <note>the solar plexus center</note>), and the same in the heart (<term>Anâhata</term> <note>the heart center</note>); five <term>ghatis</term> in the throat (<term>Visuddha</term> <note>the throat center</note>) and, lastly let him hold <italic><term>dhâranâ</term></italic> for five <term>ghatis</term> in the space between the two eye-brows (<term>Ajnâpur</term> <note>the third-eye center</note>). By this practice the elements cease to cause any harm to the great <term>Yogi</term>.

---

-> The wise one who practices the concentration of all elements, <-
-> Even by a hundred thousand lifetimes of Brahma, death does not exist for him. || 65 || <-

65. The wise <term>Yogi</term>, who thus continually practices concentration (<italic><term>dhâranâ</term></italic>), never dies through hundreds of cycles of the great <term>Brahmâ</term> <note>the creator deity; a "cycle of Brahma" represents an immense cosmic timeframe</note>.

<summary>This page describes the 'Parichaya' stage of Yoga, where the practitioner gains the ability to see and exhaust their karma through mystical means. It details specific concentration techniques on the five elements across the six chakras to achieve physical invulnerability and longevity.</summary>

<keywords>Parichaya, Karma, Kâyavyuha, Dhâranâ, Chakras, Mulâdhara, Elements, Invulnerability, Yoga practice, Pranava</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion of the stages of Yoga practice, this page describes the final stage of achievement and specific breathing techniques.</meta>

# CHAPTER III. 66-70.

### IV.—The State of Consummation <note>original: "Nishpatti"</note>

->Then, through the steady sequence of practice, the practitioner reaches the state of completion;<-
->By which, having crossed over the seeds of beginningless action, he drinks the nectar of immortality. ॥ 66 ॥<-

66. After this, through gradual exercise, the practitioner <note>A yogi is one who practices Yoga to achieve spiritual union.</note> reaches the <term>State of Consummation</term> <note>original: "Nishpatti-avasthâ"</note> (the condition of completion). The practitioner, having destroyed all the seeds of **past actions** <note>original: "karma," the law of cause and effect</note> which existed from the beginning, drinks the waters of immortality.

->When the completion of meditation occurs through one's own effort,<-
->For the steady practitioner who is peaceful and liberated while still living;<-
->When that state of perfected meditation can be reached at will,<-
->The powerful air, seizing the consciousness and the power of action,<-
->Conquers all the energy centers and dissolves into the power of knowledge. ॥ 67 ॥<-

67. When the **liberated soul** <note>original: "jivan-mukta," one who is spiritually free while still in the body</note>—the tranquil practitioner—has obtained, through practice, the consummation of <term>meditative absorption</term> <note>original: "samâdhi"</note>, and when this state of perfected meditation can be voluntarily evoked, then let the practitioner take hold of the **conscious intelligence** <note>original: "chetunâ"</note>, together with the breath, and with the <term>force of action</term> <note>original: "kriyâ-sakti"</note> conquer the six wheels <note>The chakras or energy centers of the body.</note>, and merge it into the force called <term>knowledge-power</term> <note>original: "jñâna-sakti"</note>.

->Now, for the sake of destroying afflictions, the mastery of breath must be told,<-
->By which, in this cycle of worldly existence, the cessation of experience surely occurs. ॥ 68 ॥<-

68. Now we have described the **mastery of the breath** <note>original: "vâyu-sâdhanâ"</note> in order to remove the troubles (which await the practitioner); through this knowledge vanish all sufferings and enjoyments in the circle of this universe.

->The wise one who, having placed the tongue at the root of the palate,<-
->Drinks the vital breath—for him, there is a dissolution of all practices. ॥ 69 ॥<-

69. When the skillful practitioner, by placing the tongue at the root of the palate, can drink the <term>vital breath</term> <note>original: "prâna vâyu"</note>, then there occurs complete dissolution of all Yogas (*i.e.*, he is no longer in need of further practice).*

->The wise one who drinks the cool air through the "crow-beak" contraction,<-
->Knowing the rules of the inward and outward breaths, becomes a vessel for liberation. ॥ 70 ॥<-

70. When the skillful practitioner, knowing the laws of the action of the **In-breath** <note>original: "Prâna"</note> and the **Out-breath** <note>original: "Apâna"</note>, can drink the cold air through the contraction of the mouth, in the form of a crow-bill, then he becomes entitled to liberation.

---

\* Some texts read **diseases** <note>original: "rogânâm"</note> instead of **practices** <note>original: "yogânâm"</note> in which case, it will mean "freedom from all diseases."

<summary>This page describes "Nishpatti," the final stage of Yoga where the practitioner becomes a "jivan-mukta" (liberated while living) and explains specific breathing and tongue positions used to achieve spiritual consummation.</summary>
<keywords>Consummation, Nishpatti, Samadhi, Karma, Jivan-mukta, Energy Centers, Prana, Apana, Breath Control, Liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions on specific breathing techniques (Pranayama) and mudras within the Siva Samhita, specifically focusing on the health and spiritual benefits of "drinking" air and internal secretions.</meta>

# SIVA SAMHITA.

-> **The wise one who drinks the succulent air daily according to the rules,** <-
-> **Finds that exhaustion, fever, old age, and diseases perish. || 71 ||** <-
<note>original: "सरसं यः पिबेद्वायुं... ७१"</note>

71. That wise <term>Yogi</term><note>A practitioner of Yoga.</note>, who daily drinks the <term>ambrosial air</term><note>Sanskrit: Prana. In this context, it refers to air inhaled through specific techniques believed to carry vital life force.</note>, according to proper rules, destroys fatigue, burning fever, decay, old age, and physical injuries.

-> **By turning the tongue upward and drinking the fluid from the moon,** <-
-> **The lord of Yogis certainly conquers death within a single month. || 72 ||** <-
<note>original: "रसनामूर्ध्वगां कृत्वा... ७२"</note>

72. Pointing the tongue upwards <note>This refers to Khechari Mudra, where the tongue is folded back toward the soft palate.</note>, when the Yogi can drink the <term>nectar</term><note>Sanskrit: Amrita, a divine fluid believed to secrete from the "Moon" center in the head.</note> flowing from the moon (situated between the two eye-brows), within a month he certainly would conquer death.

-> **Firmly pressing the opening of the throat and drinking according to the rule,** <-
-> **While meditating on the goddess Kundalini, one becomes a sage in six months. || 73 ||** <-
<note>original: "राजदंतबिलं गाढं... ७३"</note>

73. When having firmly closed the <term>glottis</term><note>The original term "Rajadanta-bilam" refers to the "hole of the royal tooth," a space at the back of the throat associated with the flow of internal energy.</note> by the proper yogic method, and contemplating on the goddess **<term>Kuṇḍalini</term>**<note>The primal evolutionary energy located at the base of the spine, visualized as a coiled serpent.</note>, he drinks the moon fluid of immortality, he becomes a sage or poet within six months.

-> **One should drink the air with the "crow-beak" at both twilights,** <-
-> **Focusing on the mouth of Kundalini to quiet the wasting disease. || 74 ||** <-
<note>original: "काकचंच्वा पिबेद्वायुं... ७४"</note>

74. When he drinks the air through the <term>crow-bill</term><note>Sanskrit: Kaki Mudra; the act of pursing the lips like a beak to inhale air.</note>, both in the morning and the evening twilight, contemplating that it goes to the mouth of the **Kuṇḍalini**, <term>consumption of the lungs</term><note>Also known as phthisis or tuberculosis; a common wasting disease in the 19th century.</note> is cured.

-> **The clever Yogi should drink day and night through the crow-beak;** <-
-> **By drinking the life-breath, his diseases are destroyed,** <-
-> **And he gains the powers of divine hearing and sight. || 75 ||** <-
<note>original: "अहर्निशं पिबेद्योगी... ७५"</note>

75. When the wise Yogi drinks the fluid day and night through the crow-beak, his diseases are destroyed: he acquires certainly the powers of <term>clairaudience</term><note>The ability to hear sounds from a distance or from spiritual realms.</note> and <term>clairvoyance</term><note>The ability to see things beyond the reach of the physical eyes.</note>.

-> **Pressing the teeth together, let him drink the air very slowly;** <-
-> **With the tongue turned up, the wise one soon conquers death. || 76 ||** <-
<note>original: "दंतैर्दंतान्समापीड्य... ७६"</note>

76. When firmly closing the teeth (by pressing the upper on the lower jaw), and placing the tongue upwards, the wise Yogi drinks the fluid very slowly, within a short period he conquers death.

-> **Whoever continues this practice daily for six months,** <-
-> **Is freed from all sins and destroys all diseases. || 77 ||** <-
<note>original: "षण्मासमात्रमभ्यासं... ७७"</note>

77. One, who daily continues this exercise for six months only, is freed from all sins, and destroys all diseases.

**Vocabulary:** Siva Samhita, Yogi, ambrosial air, nectar, moon, Kuṇḍalini, crow-bill, phthisis (wasting disease), clairaudience, clairvoyance, death, disease.

<summary>This page describes various yogic breathing techniques, such as Kaki Mudra and Khechari Mudra, emphasizing their power to heal diseases like tuberculosis and grant supernatural abilities such as clairvoyance.</summary>
<keywords>Siva Samhita, Pranayama, Kundalini, Kaki Mudra, Khechari Mudra, Amrita, longevity, spiritual healing, clairvoyance, clairaudience</keywords>

### CHAPTER III. 78-84.

->**By practicing for one year, he surely becomes a Bhairava.**<-
<note>original: "saṃvatsarakṛtābhyāsādbhairavo bhavati dhruvam"</note>
->**Having obtained powers such as extreme minuteness, he himself conquers the host of elements.**<-
<note>original: "aṇimādiguṇāँllabdhvā jitabhūtaganaḥ svayam"</note>

78. If he continues this exercise for a year, he becomes a <term>Bhairava</term> <note>A fierce manifestation of the god Shiva; here implying a state of supreme spiritual power.</note>; he obtains the powers of <term>Anima</term> <note>The supernatural power to become as small as an atom.</note> and others, and conquers all physical elements and the spirits associated with them.

->**By turning the tongue upward, if he remains so for even half a second,**<-
->**in an instant the Yogi is freed from disease, death, and old age.**<-

79. If the Yogi can remain for half a second with his tongue drawn upwards <note>A reference to Khechari Mudra, where the tongue is folded back into the nasal cavity.</note>, he becomes free from disease, death, and old age.

->**One should meditate on the tongue joined with the vital breath while pressing it.**<-
->**Death does not come to him; truth, truth, I have spoken.**<-

80. Verily, verily, I tell you the truth that the person never dies who contemplates by pressing the tongue, combined with the vital fluid or <term>Prana</term> <note>The vital life force or cosmic energy that flows through the body.</note>.

->**Through such practice of Yoga, he becomes like a second God of Love.**<-
->**Neither hunger, nor thirst, nor sleep, nor fainting ever arise.**<-

81. Through this exercise and Yoga, he becomes like a <term>Kamadeva</term> <note>The Hindu deity of desire and erotic love, signifying the Yogi attains a perfect, attractive physical form.</note>, without a rival. He feels neither hunger, nor thirst, nor sleep, nor swoon.

->**By this very method, the lord of Yogis upon the circle of the earth**<-
->**becomes a free-moving traveler, avoided by all calamities.**<-

82. Acting upon these methods the great Yogi becomes in the world perfectly independent; and freed from all obstacles, he can go everywhere.

->**For him there is no rebirth; he rejoices even with the gods.**<-
->**He is not smeared by merits or sins through this practice.**<-

83. By practicing thus, he is never reborn, nor is he tainted by virtue and vice <note>This refers to the transcendence of Karma, where the Yogi's actions no longer bind him to the cycle of reincarnation.</note>, but enjoys for ages with the gods.

->*The postures.*<-

->**There are eighty-four postures of various kinds.**<-
->**Taking four from among them, I speak of those told by me:**<-
->**Siddhasana, then Padmasana, Ugrasana, and Svastikasana.**<-

84. There are eighty-four postures <note>Traditionally, it is said there are 8,400,000 asanas, one for every living species, but 84 are foundational.</note>, of various modes. Out of them, four ought to be adopted, which I mention below: 1. <term>Siddhasana</term> <note>The Accomplished Pose.</note>; 2. <term>Padmasana</term> <note>The Lotus Pose.</note>; 3. <term>Ugrasana</term> <note>The Powerful or Noble Pose, often identified with Paschimottanasana.</note>; 4. <term>Svastikasana</term> <note>The Auspicious Pose.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the supernatural fruits of advanced tongue-locking practices, including immortality and immunity to hunger, before introducing the four primary physical postures of this Yoga system.</summary>
<keywords>Bhairava, Anima, Prana, Kamadeva, Immortality, Rebirth, Yoga Postures, Siddhasana, Padmasana, Ugrasana, Svastikasana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga practices from the Shiva Samhita, a foundational Sanskrit text on yoga philosophy and anatomy. We are moving into the description of specific seated postures (asanas).</meta>

# THE COMPENDIUM OF SHIVA (SIVA SAMHITA)

---

## 1.—*The Accomplished Pose (Siddhâsana)*.

> <note>original: योनिं संपीड्य यत्नेन पादमूलेन साधकः । मेढ्रोपरि पादमूलं विन्यसेद्योगवित्तदा ॥</note>
> <note>original: ऊर्ध्वं निरीक्ष्य भ्रूमध्यं निश्चलः संयतेन्द्रियः । विशेषोऽवक्रकायश्च रहस्युद्वेगवर्जितः ॥</note>
> <note>original: एतत्सिद्धासनं ज्ञेयं सिद्धानां सिद्धिदायकम् ॥ ८५ ॥</note>

85. The **Accomplished Pose** <note>original: *Siddhâsana*</note> which grants success to the practitioner is performed as follows: Pressing the heel firmly against the perineum <note>original: *yoni*</note>, the practitioner <note>original: *Yogi*</note> should place the other heel above the male organ <note>original: *lingam* or *medhra*</note>; he should fix his gaze upward on the space between the two eyebrows, remain steady, and restrain his senses. His body must be kept perfectly straight and without any bend. The location should be a secluded one, free from noise or agitation.

> <note>original: येनाभ्यासवशाच्छीघ्रं योगनिष्पत्त्तिमाप्नुयात् । सिद्धासनं सदा सेव्यं पवनाभ्यासिना परम् ॥ ८६ ॥</note>

86. One who desires to quickly reach the highest goal of Yoga through practice should adopt the **Accomplished Pose** and regularly practice the regulation of the breath <note>original: *pranayama*</note>.

> <note>original: येना संसारमुत्सृज्य लभते परमां गतिम् । नातः परतरं गुह्यतमासनं विद्यते भुवि ॥</note>
> <note>original: येनानुध्यानमात्रेण योगी पापाद्विमुच्यते ॥ ८७ ॥</note>

87. By means of this posture, the practitioner, leaving behind the world of rebirth, attains the highest state. In all the world, there is no posture more secret than this. By simply meditating while in this position, the practitioner is freed from all sin.

## 2.—*The Lotus Pose (Padmâsana)*.

> <note>original: उत्तानौ चरणौ कृत्वा ऊरुसंस्थौ प्रयत्नतः । ऊरुमध्ये तथोत्तानौ पाणी कृत्वा तु तादृशौ ॥</note>
> <note>original: नासाग्रे विन्यसेदृष्टिं दन्तमूलञ्च जिह्वया । उत्तोल्य चिबुकं वक्ष उत्थाप्य पवनं शनैः ॥</note>
> <note>original: यथाशक्त्या समाकृष्य पूरयेदुदरं शनैः । यथा शक्त्यैव पश्चात्तु रेचयेदविरोधतः ॥</note>
> <note>original: इदं पद्मासनं प्रोक्तं सर्वव्याधिविनाशनम् ॥ ८८ ॥</note>

88. I shall now describe the **Lotus Pose** <note>original: *Padmâsana*</note> which wards off or cures all diseases: Having crossed the legs, carefully place the feet on the opposite thighs (that is, the left foot on the right thigh, and vice versa); cross both the hands and place them similarly on the thighs; fix the gaze on the... <note>The text continues on the next page, detailing the focus on the tip of the nose and the positioning of the tongue and chin.</note>

<summary>This page details the instructions for two primary seated meditation postures: the Accomplished Pose (Siddhasana) and the Lotus Pose (Padmasana), emphasizing their physical alignment and spiritual benefits.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhasana, Padmasana, Accomplished Pose, Lotus Pose, Hatha Yoga, breath control, meditation postures, anatomy in yoga</keywords>

# CHAPTER III. 89-93.

...tip of the nose; pressing the tongue against the root of the teeth, (the chin should be elevated, the chest expanded) then draw the air slowly, fill the chest with all your might, and expel it slowly, in an unobstructed stream. <note>This concludes the description of a specific breathing technique begun on the previous page.</note>

->**This supreme state is difficult to attain; only some wise ones reach it. || 89 ||**<-

89. It cannot be practiced by everybody; only the wise attain success in it.

->**When the practice is performed, the breath moves rhythmically in that instant.**<-
->**Complete success will surely come to the practitioner through regular effort. || 90 ||**<-

90. By performing and practicing this posture, undoubtedly the vital airs of the practitioner at once become completely equable, and flow harmoniously through the body.

->**The yogi, established in the Lotus Pose, following the method of the vital breaths,**<-
->**Should fill the lungs; he is thus liberated—I tell you the truth, truly I tell it. || 91 ||**<-

91. Sitting in the <term>Padmâsana</term> <note>The "Lotus Pose," where the feet are placed on opposite thighs.</note> posture, and knowing the action of the <term>Prâṇa</term> <note>The upward-moving vital life force.</note> and <term>Apâna</term> <note>The downward-moving vital force.</note>, when the Yogi performs the regulation of the breath, he is emancipated. I tell you the truth. Verily, I tell you the truth.

### 3.—The *Ugrâsana*.

->**Stretch out both legs, keeping them separate from one another;**<-
->**Holding them firmly with both hands, place the head upon the knees.**<-
->**This is called the Stern Pose; it kindles the internal fire of the breath,**<-
->**Removes the exhaustion of the body, and is known as the Posterior Stretch.**<-
->**The wise man who masters this excellent posture every day,**<-
->**The breath certainly moves through his posterior path. || 92 ||**<-

92. Stretch out both the legs and keep them apart; firmly take hold of the head by the hands, and place them on the knees. <note>In modern yoga, this is usually performed by grabbing the toes rather than the head, though the goal remains touching the head to the knees.</note> This is called <term>Ugrâsana</term> (the stern-posture); it excites the motion of the air, destroys the dullness and uneasiness of the body, and is also called <term>Paschima-uttâna</term> (the posterior stretch posture). That wise man who daily practices this noble posture can certainly induce the flow of the air through the rear passage. <note>original: "per viam posteriori". This refers to the breath entering the Sushumna Nadi, or the central spiritual channel of the spine.</note>

->**For those devoted to this practice, all spiritual perfections arise.**<-
->**Therefore, the Yogi should diligently master this for his own success. || 93 ||**<-

93. Those who practice this obtain all the <term>siddhis</term> <note>Extraordinary psychic or spiritual powers attained through yoga.</note>; therefore, those desirous of attaining powers should practice this diligently.

<meta>This page continues the instructions on yogic practices from the Siva Samhita, transitioning from general secrecy of the teachings to the specific technical instructions for the Svastikāsana posture.</meta>

# THE COMPENDIUM OF SHIVA

<page-num>40</page-num>

->**This must be preserved with great effort and never shared with just anyone.**<-
->**By this, the mastery of the vital breath—which destroys a flood of miseries—is quickly attained. || 94 ||**<-

94. This should be kept secret with the greatest care, and not be given to anybody and everybody. Through it, <term>vāyu-siddhi</term> <note>original: vāyu-siddhi; the power or "perfection" of the breath, referring to the yogic mastery over the body's internal vital energies.</note> is easily obtained, and it destroys a multitude of miseries.

### 4. The Auspicious Posture (Svastikāsana)

->**Having properly placed the soles of both feet between the knees and the thighs,**<-
->**one sits at ease with the body straight; this is declared the Svastika. || 95 ||**<-

95. Place the soles of the feet completely under the thighs, keep the body straight, and sit at ease. This is called the <term>Svastikāsana</term> <note>Literally the "Auspicious" or "Well-being" posture, often associated with the cross-symbol of the same name representing prosperity.</note>.

->**By this method, the wise practitioner should master the vital breath.**<-
->**No disease can then attack the body, and the mastery of the air is achieved. || 96 ||**<-

96. In this way, the wise <term>Yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of the path of Yoga.</note> should practise the regulation of the air. No disease can attack his body, and he obtains <term>vāyu siddhi</term> <note>Mastery of the breath and vital energy.</note>.

->**This is called the Easy Seat, the destroyer of all suffering.**<-
->**The Svastika, the highest means of creating well-being, should be kept secret by practitioners. || 97 ||**<-

97. This is also called the <term>Sukhāsana</term> <note>Literally the "Pleasant" or "Easy" seat.</note>, the easy posture. This health-giving, good <term>Svastikāsana</term> should be kept secret by the <term>Yogi</term>.

<summary>This page describes the importance of keeping yogic secrets and provides the technical instructions for Svastikāsana (the Auspicious Posture). It emphasizes that mastering this seat leads to the regulation of breath and the prevention of disease.</summary>
<keywords>Siva Samhita, vāyu-siddhi, breath mastery, Svastikāsana, Auspicious Posture, Sukhāsana, Easy Posture, Hatha Yoga, yoga secrecy</keywords>
